# Coverage ledger

Two different measures. Do not confuse them.

**Documented / audited** = a system document in this database explains it to the standard in
[`EVIDENCE-STANDARD.md`](EVIDENCE-STANDARD.md). **This is the goal.**

**Classified** = the GFZ ledger. A function carries a `PrimaryOwner`, `FinalNoPrimaryOwner`,
or `CodeDomain` tag. Machine-verifiable, regenerated by
[`ReportV121Coverage.java`](../../Tools/ghidra/scripts/ReportV121Coverage.java) — **tracked in
git as of 2026-09-08; until then this line named a file the repository did not contain.**
**This is a progress instrument, not the goal** — it tells you what is left to understand.

A function can be classified and still completely un-understood — `NativeRoutine.BoundedLoop.DirectCallOnly`
is an honest shape label, not knowledge.

## Definition of done

The project is complete when **all** of these hold:

1. Unresolved = **0** in the GFZ ledger, reached without generic filler.
2. Every generic shape class has been replaced by, or supplemented with, a real mechanism
   identity — no function is merely "accounted for".
3. Every system in every category is **`AUDITED`** — survived a recorded falsification pass,
   not merely `DOCUMENTED`.
4. [`OPEN-QUESTIONS.md`](OPEN-QUESTIONS.md) is **empty**.
4b. **Thirty consecutive audit passes find nothing** in one uninterrupted run — see
   [`FINAL-AUDIT-PROTOCOL.md`](FINAL-AUDIT-PROTOCOL.md).
5. [`CALL-CHAINS.md`](CALL-CHAINS.md) covers every cross-subsystem transition, in both
   directions, with owner transitions distinguished from triggers.
6. The complete proven V121 delta replays deterministically onto a fresh import of canonical
   V120 (`507490534a8bb67e8d3c6deba4d527c23ab9fdd3672494e511db0d9ef0e511a9`), packs, reopens
   and reproduces — after which V121 may be declared canonical.

## What the completion metric cannot see

`progress.py`'s `engine_explained` component counts **functions cited by a document**: 576 of 34,270,
or 1.68 %. That is deliberately strict and it is the honest floor, but it under-reports one real kind
of progress.

Pass 52 explained roughly **12,468 functions as categories** rather than individually:

| | functions | how |
|---|---:|---|
| glue-shaped, self-explaining by size | ~5,283 | thunks (mean **5 B**), `NativeLeaf` (**14 B**), lifecycle and compiler-generated glue |
| reachable only through a data-table pointer | 7,185 | measured: address-taken among `.rdata`'s 27,392 pointer slots |

The mission's bar is *"nothing unexplainable"*, and "these 5,283 are `JMP rel32` forwarders" meets
that bar for those 5,283 without a document citing each one.

**The metric is not being changed to count them.** Adding a category-credit component mid-mission
would raise the number without raising the understanding, which is the failure mode the standard
forbids. The gap is recorded here instead: **the headline percentage is a floor, and it is known to
be one.**


## Writing lowers `audit_coverage` by construction

**2026-09-03.** `audit_coverage` reached 100 % and then fell to 96.94 % within the hour — **not because
anything regressed, but because three documents were written.** Every new document is `+1` to that
denominator and arrives owing a first pass.

> **The two terms are in tension by construction.** `engine_explained` moves by writing;
> `audit_coverage` falls when you do. A run that only writes will watch the audit figure decay, and a
> run that only audits will watch the deliverable stall — which is exactly the trade the whole night
> has been managing between them.

**So the writing phase must budget for the pass, and the budget is not optional:**

- **A new document owes a first pass before its author starts another one.** Nothing enforces this;
  the scorer only counts, and 100 % is reachable by not writing.
- **The author does not own that pass.** Two sessions recused from their own documents tonight on
  measured grounds — one because it had been corrected twice by adjacent work, both times surviving
  their own adversarial pass. **A pass by the author is the weakest available check and both of them
  said so before being asked.**
- **A first pass is cheap and finds the cheap errors** — fabricated sizes, mis-scoped absences, counts
  right for the wrong population, a table read six entries deep. **Eight of the night's thirteen
  first passes overturned something.** The expected yield is high enough that skipping one is a
  choice, not an oversight.

**Current writing debt: two documents, both written this hour.**

| document | lines | owes |
|---|---:|---|
| `the-282-command-parameter-console.md` | 296 | first pass |
| `the-post-processing-effect-catalogue.md` | 844 | first pass |

**And both have a stated attack surface already**, handed over by their own authors — which is the
cheapest possible starting point for whoever takes them:

- The console: the `step` column of its extraction is **labelled unreliable by its author**, with the
  tell (58 rows `-0.01`, none `+0.01`). Its `kind` column reproduces an independently published
  distribution and is sound.
- The catalogue: three claims at **three different support levels**, stated in its §8 — 325 names for
  the suffix grammar, **four literals at two of seven helpers** for the multiplier reading, and **one
  name pair** for the `fParam_FocusDistance` link. **The third is the one to attack.**

## The `questions_closed` metric has never read `OPEN-QUESTIONS.md`

**Measured 2026-09-03, not estimated.** The scorer counts markdown checkboxes:

```
open_q   += len(re.findall(r"^\s*-\s*\[ \]", t, re.M))
closed_q += len(re.findall(r"^\s*-\s*\[x\]", t, re.M))
```

**`OPEN-QUESTIONS.md` contains zero checkboxes.** Its **383 verdict-bearing rows and 97 staged
proposals are outside both the numerator and the denominator.** The `128 / 665` the metric reports is
entirely per-document §7 checkbox lists — real questions, but not the register the metric is named
after.

> **A metric named after the register that does not read the register.** Same family as the audit
> field that counted amendments and the one that never read `falsification_passes` — the third and
> fourth corrections to this scorer were both about *what a field is allowed to prove*; this one is
> about *what the field is pointed at*, which no amount of tightening the pattern would have found.

**It was found by acting on the number rather than reading it.** Fifteen proposal rows were restated
from `untriaged` to a real verdict, and `questions_closed` moved by **one** — a change that large
producing a change that small is the discrepancy that opened the file.

**Not patched in the same pass that found it, deliberately.** Counting the register means deciding
whether `PARTLY` / `HALF ANSWERED` are closed (they are not), and whether the merge-derived
`**ANSWERED**` rows — whose state was inferred from prose by a script — may count at all (they may
not, or the metric asserts a check that never ran, which is the defect this project has now corrected
in four separate fields). **Both decisions change the number more than the bug does**, so they get
their own pass with a control, not a patch appended to the discovery.

### Patched, with a control, and the untrusted pile turned out to be empty

**`questions_closed` now reads both.** Per-document sections **128 closed / 537 open**; the register
**181 / 534**; total **309 / 1,380**, against `128 / 665` before. The component moved **19.25 % →
22.39 %** — *upward*, because the register is better answered than the checkbox lists, which is the
opposite of what a metric that had been ignoring it would suggest.

**Both conservative calls held:** `PARTLY` / `PARTIAL` / `HALF ANSWERED` count **open** (a question
half answered is a question), and a row with no verdict at all counts **open** rather than being
skipped. `--self-test` covers four cases, including an **escaped `\|` inside a cell**, which naive
splitting would have turned into a phantom column.

**And the guard fired zero times, for a reason worth recording.** Four rows carry the merge's bare
`**ANSWERED**`; all four sit beside staging text that itself begins *"ANSWERED — …"* with substance,
so each was counted from its own text and none from the inferred column.

> **The untrusted pile is empty, and that is a result rather than a relief:** in every case where the
> merge's guess could be checked against the text it was drawn from, it agreed. **That does not make
> the guess trustworthy — it makes the four rows checkable.** The guard stays, because it is what
> distinguishes those two things.

## Located is not read — 23 functions the database has built arguments on top of and never opened

**A new checker, from a failure a session found by hand.** `BattleStats_HasRequiredLicenseForAction`
was **named, located, cited — and never read**, next to a section stating flatly that the layout was
not established. **Every gate passed on it**: the address resolved, the link worked, the name was in
the index.

**The signal is cheap.** A function that was actually read carries **interior** address citations — an
instruction anchor, a branch, a store, the offset a field is read at. A function that was merely
located gets its entry cited and nothing else.

```
6,582 functions cited          6,463 by their entry only (98.2 %)
```

**That headline number is not the finding, and reporting it as one would be the inflation defect.**
Most citations live in bulk tables — one RTTI catalogue alone supplies thousands of entry-only rows,
correctly. Size alone over-reports too: a document whose *subject* is the unread legitimately lists
big bodies nobody has opened.

**The cut that has no excuse is "leaned on and never opened" — cited by five or more documents, body
≥ 300 bytes, no interior anchor anywhere. 23 functions.**

| function | size | docs |
|---|---:|---:|
| `0026D910` `WorldField_InitializeCurrentFieldRuntimeParams` | 418 B | **10** |
| `002F7380` | 723 B | 9 |
| **`0030F760` `BattleUnit_CommitAction`** | **570 B** | **9** |
| `002AAE40` | 2,440 B | 8 |
| `0020AC30` | 572 B | 8 |
| `002AC5F0` | 7,489 B | 7 |
| `002F8060` `BattleUnit_TryTriggerNearbyAuthoredFieldActivation` | 648 B | 7 |

> **These are functions the database has already built arguments on top of.** A name cited by nine
> documents is load-bearing whether or not anyone opened it, and **the citation count is what makes
> the omission expensive rather than merely incomplete.**

**`BattleUnit_CommitAction` is the sharpest**: nine documents cite the commit point for every action
in combat, and not one cites an instruction inside it.

**Kept as a suspicion, not a defect, and the tool exits 0.** The false positives are real and
predictable — a small function whose behaviour fits in a sentence, a function cited only to say
something *about* it, a genuine read whose anchors live in a decompiled listing rather than in
backticked addresses. **A checker that called these defects would be asserting a check that never
ran, which is the failure it exists to catch.**

## The function list is an instrument with coverage, not ground truth

**A session's caller census reported that nothing writes request kind `2`, making a live branch look
like dead code — and it was one sentence from being written up that way.** An unbounded `.text` scan
found a **tenth write site inside a 27-byte function that is absent from `functions.tsv`**, and that
function is **the only writer of kind 2.**

> **A caller census is bounded by its function list. This one was wrong by exactly one function, and
> the missing function was the only writer of the value in question.**

**`verify_doc_addresses` independently flagged the same five instruction addresses as `.text`
non-function — correct, and for exactly the same reason the census missed them.** The flag was
**dispositioned rather than cleared**: removing the citation would have deleted the only record of the
only writer of kind 2. **Two instruments reporting the same gap is evidence, not two problems.**

### Measured, because the consequence lands on this project's own scorer

```
listed functions            34,270          7,429,947 bytes
span 00121000-008C1635                      7,996,981 bytes
between listed bodies       31,933 regions    568,793 bytes   (7.11 % of the span)
   of which <= 64 bytes     31,518 regions    252,440 bytes
largest gaps                00133BBF +81,729 · 0014C4E6 +50,778 · 0089BF02 +36,009
```

**`engine_explained` counts cited *listed* functions over *listed* functions**, and **a citation
landing in a gap is silently dropped** — so the metric **under-counts by an unmeasured amount.** Most
of the small gaps are alignment padding, but 252 KB of them is not nothing, and the 27-byte function
proves at least one is real code.

**The scorer now prints its own function-list coverage on every run.** The number is not being
changed — **the name is being made honest.** `engine_explained` measures what the database explains of
*the functions the instrument knows about*, which is a different and smaller claim than its name.

> **Standing note for every session: "no caller writes X" means "no *listed* function writes X" until
> an unbounded scan agrees.**

The same unbounded scan produced one false positive in the same run — a float read decoded
mid-instruction. **That is the standing price for completeness, and it is worth paying here**: a false
positive is discarded by reading it, while a false negative silently becomes a claim about dead code.

## audit_coverage 84.30 % -> 91.74 %, and none of it was a stamp

**Nineteen documents carried no falsification pass; eleven were the console block** — the nine arm
slices plus their frame and work order. **That block has been attacked harder than anything else in
the database and none of it was recorded in the documents themselves.** The evidence was in the audit
log; the documents said `passes: 0`.

**The fix was to write what actually happened to each slice, not to stamp them.** Every line is an
attack that ran and a result it produced, and they differ per slice because the attacks did:

- **A** — the join's one real contradiction landed *here*, and **this slice's row was the wrong one**;
  the correction carried the trap that amends the width rule.
- **B** — **the control.** Zero instances of the third direction mechanism against D's 25 and E's 21;
  **without a clean slice read by the same method, D's 25 was indistinguishable from an artefact.**
- **C** — the label `Camera Over Write` corroborating a behaviour already read from the arm.
- **D** — **a correction sent to it was wrong**; D had the mechanism first, as its headline.
- **E** — id 166 giving property 14 **before slice I's rule existed**.
- **F** — **an instrument said this document was wrong and the document was right**: seven float
  steppers, not zero.
- **G** — **broke the claim made about it**: the mechanism was not confined to D and E.
- **H** — **tested a published prediction and the prediction failed**, which is why 11/19/20/21 are
  now known to be reached by nothing.
- **I** — its central rule **proved from the callee side**, and its "second shared tail" retracted.

> **A slice that survived nothing gets a section saying so. Every one of these survived something
> specific, and the section names it.**

## A false positive on the "leaned on" cut, fired and fixed the right way

`0022A0B0` sat on the cut with **seven citations, all at its entry** — and it had been read
thoroughly. The reader simply had no occasion to cite an interior address. An independent
re-derivation confirmed the published memory budget in full and then **supplied the interior addresses
so it would stop being a false positive.**

> **The cure for a false positive on this cut is not to relax the cut; it is to cite the evidence the
> document already rests on.** An interior anchor makes a row checkable rather than merely stated —
> which is what the cut was asking for all along.

## The cut was inflated by documents that cite everything — corrected, and it was my own instrument

**A second false-positive mode**, found by use: `007E5160` had six citing documents and **five were
table rows in a 2,645-line vtable index**, which gives it a class name and a slot number and claims
nothing about its behaviour.

> **"Cited by N documents" is satisfied by an index, and an index does not lean on anything.**

**Excluding documents that cite more than 300 distinct functions dropped the cut from 23 to zero.**
Six documents pass that threshold — `ADDRESS-INDEX.md` (6,507, **generated from the documents
themselves**), the vtable index (4,276), a PhyreEngine accessor catalogue (753), **`AUDIT-LOG.md`
(458)**, `OPEN-QUESTIONS.md` (342) and the generated console names table (318).

**Two of those are aggregates of the corpus, so every function they mention was being counted as
leaning on itself.** `ADDRESS-INDEX.md` is built *by scanning the documents*; `AUDIT-LOG.md` is the
running record of the work. **A citation there is not a dependency; it is an echo.**

> **The inflation defect, in an instrument I built to find the absence defect.** A cut that "agrees
> suspiciously with itself" is exactly what cross-category comparison is supposed to catch, and it took
> another session hitting a row for anyone to look.

**Corrected population, honestly restated:**

```
cited by 5+ real documents    0
cited by 4+ real documents    2
cited by 3+ real documents   19      <- the honest equivalent of the original cut
cited by 2+ real documents   83
```

**The default is now 3, and the report prints both counts** — `4 docs (6 incl. indexes)` — so the
inflation is visible rather than removed. **The functions cleared under the old threshold were real
clearances**; what was wrong was the claim about how many remained.

**And the weak citation earned its keep.** The index row is what made `007E5160` worth reading at all:
**a class name and a slot number are a far better starting point than an address.** The fix is to stop
counting index rows as *leaning*, not to stop valuing them.

## Three methods that share an assumption are one method

A session was one draft away from publishing that the Mersenne Twister **is never explicitly seeded**,
with **three independent checks agreeing on zero**: a caller census of the body, an unbounded scan for
`E8` call encodings targeting it, and a scan for 8-byte pointers to it anywhere in the image.

**All three were wrong together.** The name is attached to the **thunk**, not to the body — the call
graph *does* record both callers, indexed under the thunk's address.

> **Each check asked "what calls the body", and nothing does: the thunk jumps to it.**
> **Independence has to be independence of the question, not just of the technique.**

**This is the control lesson one level up.** *A control can only certify the axis it varies* was about
one instrument; this is about three, and the shared axis was **the question**, not the method.

**Standing rule, extended:** when a census returns **zero**, vary the *question*, not just the tool —
and **check whether the name you queried sits on the body or on a thunk**, because in this image it is
sometimes the latter.

## The checker could not see evidence in the form evidence is usually written in

**Third correction to the same instrument in one tick, and this one inverted its numbers.**

The cut counted only **backticked** addresses. **A document whose evidence is a disassembly listing
writes its interior addresses inside a fence, unbackticked** — which is the most likely place an
interior anchor will ever live. **Every such document looked unread**, and the first to expose it was
this checker's own author's: a document citing seven interior addresses of a function was still
reporting that function as never opened.

> **The instrument could not see the evidence in the form the evidence is usually written in.**

**Fixed by reading bare 8-hex tokens inside fences as well as backticked ones anywhere — and the
number moved in both directions.** Functions whose anchors were in listings dropped out; functions
whose *citing documents* had only ever cited them from inside a fence entered the population.
**23 again, from a different 23.**

### And the cause was a standing rule of mine, broken for the third time tonight

The patch was applied through a **shell heredoc**, which turned `\b` into a literal **backspace
byte**. The regex became `\x08(0[0-9A-Fa-f]{7})\x08` — **a pattern that can never match anything**,
silently returning zero and leaving the cut looking merely unchanged.

**There is a written note saying to write escaped strings with an editor and never through a shell
heredoc.** It has now been broken three times in one session: NUL bytes into two documents, NUL bytes
into the NUL checker three minutes later, and this.

> **A rule that lives in a note and not in the tooling is a rule you will break while fixing the thing
> it warned you about** — which is the same sentence this project wrote the first time, and the note
> is still not wired into anything.

### The rate worth planning against

Of eight cut entries examined by one session: **five genuinely unread, two already read to tier 1, one
inflated by index citations but genuinely unread.**

> **Two in eight were already read.** Not a complaint about the cut — both confirmations were worth
> having and both now carry interior anchors — **but a number to have before anyone plans the
> remaining seventeen as if they were all unopened.**

## One dword holding a mode and flags

`002ABF70` dispatches on the **low byte of a dword at `+0xB0`** through a jump table at `002AC1B4`,
**four bytes past its own `ret`** — the same construction as the console dispatcher.

> **A reader who treats `+0xB0` as a byte gets the right mode and silently loses bit 22** — which one
> mode's body is the only place to read it.

**Same species as `+0x24` on two bases and `+0x0E` across two passes: correct about the thing you
asked, wrong about the thing you did not.**

## Computed operands: three instances, one shape

| a scan keyed on | misses |
|---|---|
| the memory operand of `addss` | a constant held in a register — slice F ids 173/174 |
| `mov edx, <imm>` | the property id via `xor esi,esi` + `lea edx,[rsi+imm]` |
| `mov ecx, <literal>` | **two of nine heap ids** in the boot table, via `lea ecx,[r9-0xE]` |

> **All three are one shape: the operand is computed rather than encoded, and the scan reads the
> encoding.**

## The rule is wired into the tooling now, and it found a fourth instance on its first run

**`check_source_hygiene.py`** scans every script and document for **C0 control bytes** other than tab,
newline and carriage return. Those are the residue of a shell mangling an escape sequence, and **every
one of them is invisible in a terminal.**

**It ships a self-test built from the exact failure that motivated it** — a `\b` turned into a literal
backspace inside a regex literal, which is **not a syntax error**: the pattern simply matches nothing,
forever, and returns zero without complaint.

**First run over 212 files found one occurrence, and it is the best possible one.**

> A published document contained a **real backspace byte** in the sentence
> *"`audit_crossrefs.py`'s `BODYSIZE` pattern ended in `\b`…"* — **a passage explaining a mangled
> pattern, mangled the same way, by the same mechanism, while being written.**

**That is the fourth instance in one session** — NUL into two documents, NUL into the NUL checker three
minutes later, a backspace into a live regex, and now a backspace into the prose describing the first
one. Repaired; the scan is clean across all 212 files.

> **A rule that lives in a note and not in the tooling is a rule you will break while fixing the thing
> it warned you about.** The project wrote that sentence the first time this happened and left it in
> prose. **This is the same sentence, wired to an exit code.**

## A third false-positive shape, and the most common honest case

`0025D9E0` was cited by four documents, **every one from a call chain or an ownership table** —
*"runs the transaction, does not own weather"*, *"a per-frame field pass"*.

> **Its role was known from the call graph and its body had never been read. Edges known, body
> unopened** — neither read-but-uncited nor cited-by-an-index, and **probably the most common honest
> case on the cut.**

**What the read added that no call-graph citation could carry:**

- **An actor's script is driven repeatedly inside one frame.** The updater is called, its result
  tested for *settled* (`0x40`) and *work pending* (`0x0F`), and **called again in the same frame with
  no iteration cap** — the cap's absence measured against the other three loops in the same function,
  which do have them. **So a scripted event can start, run several statements and reach a wait in the
  frame you triggered it** — and **anything hooking that updater is called several times per actor per
  frame. A hook counting frames from the callee is simply wrong.**
- **A save in flight stops the entire pass — and it still returns `1`.** A caller cannot distinguish
  *ran* from *declined because saving* by the return value.
- **`+0x11` bit 0 is republished from `+0x10` bit 7 every frame**, so a reader sampling one byte sees
  a stale copy of the other.
- **Byte `+0x14` has two independent halves** — bit `0x40` settled, bits `0x0F` pending — and two
  whole-actor passes **fold every actor's `+0x14` into a running AND**: *"is everything settled"*
  computed by conjunction rather than by a counter.

**And a refusal worth keeping:** the four-iteration rotating single-bit mask was **not** connected to
the gambit enable mask. *Same idiom, different structure.*

## "Clean" and "scanned nothing" read identically

The new hygiene checker was run by its first user against **a file path**. It walks directories, so it
matched nothing and printed:

```
no stray control bytes in 0 file(s)
```

> **A clean result over an empty population is not a clean result** — and the only reason anyone
> noticed is that the tool prints its population beside its number, which is this project's own rule
> firing **inside a checker rather than inside a document.**

**Fixed twice over:** a file path is now accepted, and **an empty population is an error with its own
exit code**, not a sentence that has to be read carefully.

## Ranking the cut by the gap between body and claim

**The fourth shape — read for one purpose, rest of the body unexamined — is invisible to every gate,
because the part that was read is correct.** The only signal is **the size of the body against the
size of the claim**, and that can be measured rather than judged.

The cut now counts **lines of prose citing documents spend on each address** and ranks by **bytes per
line**:

```
002A8C50   3363 B   1121 B/line   numeric-formatting
005E5400   4088 B   1022 B/line   member-registration
005AD8F0   5580 B    697 B/line   member-registration
0035BC50   1014 B    338 B/line   battle-runtime-mode-ladder
002FEF30   2859 B    285 B/line   BattleStats_RebuildEffectiveStatsAndAffinityState
```

**On its first run it found a gap whose own author had already declared it.** `002FEF30` is the
function a session marked *"PARTIAL on purpose — I read its prologue and base-stat entry, not its
contribution formula"*. **The heuristic independently ranked it fifth of twenty-two.**

> **It is not decisive and is not meant to be. It ranks the cut by where the fourth shape hides.**

### The cut is a frontier, not a backlog

**It rose from 21 to 22 while three entries were being cleared**, because opening a function adds its
callees to the citation graph.

> **The list does not shrink monotonically and should never be reported as if it did.** A run that
> clears three and adds two has still moved.

## The reflex was worth more than the note

A session reached `0025DF00` by following **its own citation**, and inside it:

```asm
0025DFC6  lea rsi, [rax + rcx*8]     ; an array element - NOT the actor record
```

**`+0x14` on `rsi` is written `= 1` at four sites; `+0x14` on `rdi` is the actor record's flag byte.**
The four writes are **the obvious candidate for "what sets the pending bit"** and are a different
structure entirely — **third instance of the `+0x24`-on-two-bases species, and the first caught before
it reached a document.**

> **The only reason it was checked is that mapping the argument registers had become reflex after
> writing that trap up. The reflex was worth more than the note.**

**And the follow-up was a refinement, not a retraction:** the caller tests bit `0x40` and the low
nibble; this function tests `0x40` and **`0x10`**, which is outside the nibble — so **the byte has at
least three separately-tested regions.** The earlier reading stands and was simply not the whole byte.

## A second ranking, from instructions rather than from prose

**Wired in from a census delivered by another session** — all 38,161 functions with a body, in 2.8
seconds, **zero unreadable**, so the population is the whole ledger rather than a subset.

> **A large SSE-move count against almost no backward jumps is never a processing pass** — it is a
> large structure snapshotted to the stack, modified and written back.

**The two rankings are close to orthogonal, and that is the useful case: bytes-per-line finds what we
under-described; SSE-per-loop finds what we mis-typed.** The stat-cap function that bytes-per-line
ranked fifth is **0 % SSE with 4 loops** — invisible to the second column entirely.

**Its first act was to reproduce a family somebody had already documented, from instructions alone:**
573 functions are SSE-heavy and loop-free, and the top of that list is the `004xxxxx` generated
float-table builders. **That is the validation to want before trusting a ranking on an undocumented
function.**

### But it is a discovery list, not a debt list

**The top entries are cited by zero documents.** The leaned-on cut measures *debt* — functions the
database has built arguments on. This one measures *shape* — and it surfaces functions nobody has
touched at all, which is a different and complementary job.

**And it has its own false-positive profile.** `001F0AD0` appears on it and **has been read**, in
detail, by the session that identified its eight shadow-map matrices — the document simply cites it at
its entry. **The same read-but-uncited mode as the first cut, in a list built from completely
different evidence.**

### A population note that must travel with any figure from it

`sse_moves` counts **`movups` `movss` `movaps` `movdqu` `movdqa` `movupd` `movapd`**. Three hand
counts of the same functions disagreed with it — 208 vs 225, 311 vs 336, 162 vs 162 —

> **and the two "mismatches" are different populations, not different measurements**: one hand count
> was `movups` only, the other `movups`+`movss`.

**Do not quote a census figure beside a hand count without saying which population each is.**

## A fifth instrument limit: a dynamic stack frame distorts every local in the listing

`0035BC50` was taken from the ranked cut and **not written up, on purpose.**

Its prologue calls a stack-probe helper, negates the result, and **adds that value to every stack
reference in the body**:

```c
lVar8 = FUN_00808F10();  lVar8 = -lVar8;
...  *(uint *)(&stack0x00000060 + lVar8)  ...
```

**Every local in the decompiled listing is therefore addressed through a runtime-computed offset**,
and the decompiler renders them as symbolic `&stack0x…` expressions rather than as named variables. A
reader treating those as fixed slots is reading **the decompiler's model of a frame that does not
exist at a fixed size.**

> **This is the fourth way this executable's decompiled output has misdescribed a function, and the
> first that distorts *locals* rather than a return type or an extent.** Three earlier ones were about
> the return; one was about the body's boundary; this one is about everything inside it.

**CORRECTED 2026-09-03 — the frame is fixed, and the one measurement made from the listing was wrong.**

> `0035BC54  mov eax, 0x1128` — **a compile-time constant** — then `call _alloca_probe`, and an
> epilogue `add rsp, 0x1128` with the stack cookie at a fixed `+0x1100`. **This is MSVC's large-frame
> stack probe, mandatory for any frame over one page. It is not `alloca`.**
>
> **Censused image-wide: 34 functions use the constant-size probe and 0 use a non-constant size. This
> executable contains no dynamically sized stack frame at all.**

**So the consequence runs opposite to the warning above.** The locals are at fixed offsets and the
listing merely cannot prove it — **the distortion is cosmetic, over an enumerable set of 34
functions.** *A reader who distrusts the frame size loses the whole function; a reader who trusts the
listing loses only confidence.* **The instrument limit is real and is the mildest of the five, not the
worst.**

**And the listing produced exactly the error it invites.** This section first said *"four slots at
stride `0x60`"*. **The stride is `0xC0`:**

```asm
0035BDB0  lea rsi, [rdi - 0x60]    ; two cursors into the SAME slot
0035BE53  add rdi, 0xC0            ; the stride
```

> **`rsi` and `rdi` are two views of one slot sixty bytes apart, and a listing showing both invites
> reading their difference as the stride.** The slots are at `022C8080`, `022C8140`, `022C8200`,
> `022C82C0`.

**What the function does, measured from the image rather than the listing:** four slots at stride
`0xC0`, gated by four independent conditions — **two of them per-mode rather than per-flag, so "which
flag suppresses the pass" had no answer as posed** — publishing a selection state into
`022C8060 … 022C8078`, where the selected slot is **the one whose packed actor handle at `slot+0x04`
equals the current party focus handle.**

**What was not done: a document.** The claims a reader would want — which slot is selected, what the
counters count, which flag suppresses the pass — all rest on those distorted locals.

> **A function whose decompilation is distorted by a dynamic frame needs an image reader, not a
> decompiler**, and handing it to one is cheaper than publishing a reading that cannot be defended.

**This is the reverse of the "leaned on" cut's usual failure.** There the citation looked stronger than
the read; here the *listing* looks stronger than it is. **Both are cases of an instrument's output
being more confident than its evidence.**

## There is no hunt system, and the way that was established is the point

**Zero occurrences of `bestiary` in 48 MB. One `hunt` — inside `"onSessionDisconnected ServerShuntdown"`.
One `Hunt` — an achievement title. One `clan` — a debug-menu label from this database's own console
work. Two functions in the entire image carry the category's owner tag.**

> **Every string hit was read rather than counted.** A count would have reported *"hunt: 1"* and been
> misleading **in the opposite direction from usual** — the single match is a **misspelling**, not a
> subsystem.

**The negative has a positive half, which is what makes it an answer rather than a gap:** the native
surface is **six flag banks with a uniform VM query/setter each**, two event-memory lookups, and a
~20-kind statistics counter already met from the gil side. **A hunt is a flag convention plus a
counter, authored in script over that surface.**

### The category list was written from the game, and the engine is not organised that way

**This is the fourth time this run that "where is the X system" resolved to "that is one field on a
table everything else already uses"** — after equipment un-apply, item effect resolution, and the party
leader.

> **The categories were named for things a player can point at. The engine is few mechanisms and many
> fields**, so a category can be thin because the work is done and there is nothing native to find.
> **A thin category is not automatically an unworked one, and the coverage table cannot tell the
> difference.**

**`14-Story-Event-Quest-Hunt` is now complete at 42 cited addresses**, and its completeness looks
identical to neglect in every measurement this project makes.

> ### 2026-09-08 — and the last measurable gap is closed too
>
> **All 15 functions the ledger assigns to `Story_Event_Quest_Hunt` have now been read individually** — charter disposition **A** for a whole owned population, a first here. The block that did it read exactly one new function, `00379160`; **the other two outstanding rows had been read weeks earlier and the address table had never been told.**
>
> That sharpens the warning above rather than lifting it. `cov%` for this category read **100.0 before and after**, and the paragraph above already said the measurement cannot see the difference between a finished category and an abandoned one. **It also cannot see the difference between a finished category and one that merely says it is.** The instrument that found the stale rows was enumerating the owned set and checking each row against the prose — nothing automated.
>
> **Still L1.** No contract, no `_types/` entry, no runtime evidence. Complete at the function level is not reconstruction-ready, and this ledger does not let one stand in for the other.

## Two event-memory lookups, consulted back to back, with different shapes

`ActorManager_BindLoadedDefinitionPayload` queries **two** structures on the same definition id:

- a **linear `uint16` search** — count at `+8`, entries from `+0xA` — which returns **absent**, not
  present, *and the polarity is load-bearing at the call site*;
- a **table directly indexed by the definition id** into 3-byte records, count at `+4`, records from
  `+8`. **No search.**

> **One asks "is this id in a list?", the other asks "what does this id's record say?" A list that
> grows and a table sized to the id space are different kinds of memory** — and the engine consults
> both before binding an actor.

**The list is populated at area load, not when an event occurs** — one of its two writers is owned by
`WorldAreaField`.

## A session audited its own published work against a hazard found later

Warned that a flag census is a register-tracking job, a session **checked which of its own instruments
were exposed** rather than only guarding the new work. **One was not block-aware — the one that
produced a published band census.** Patched, re-run: **`0x1A` still 7 sites, `0x1B` still 28, same
owner distribution.**

> **The published claim survived, and it has now been *checked* rather than assumed.** The warning
> cost one command and bought a verified document.

**That is the right response to a new hazard class: not only "guard the next block" but "which already-shipped numbers came from an instrument with this shape?"**

## Two sessions read the same function at the same time, and it was the coordinator's fault

**`002A9F00` was opened twice, independently, within one tick.** The coordinator took it from the
ranked cut **without announcing it** — every other assignment tonight was routed by message, and this
one was not.

> **The collision was not a protocol gap; it was the coordinator exempting himself from the protocol.**

**No document was duplicated** — the helper's landed, the coordinator's was never written — and the
accident produced something worth keeping: **an unplanned independent re-derivation**, with a real
disagreement in it.

**Where the two reads agree**, having been produced by a decompiler and by an image reader with no
contact:

- the function opens by **snapshotting the render state to its frame in unrolled `movups` pairs** —
  the idiom the SSE column ranked it for;
- it loads **`movabs 0x4100400000000000`**, the same tag-class constant decoded in the markup parser;
- **`context + 0x10E4` is decremented once per character.**

**Where they disagree, and the disagreement is the useful part.** One read calls `+0x10E4` **a
remaining width budget**; the other reads it as **a fade factor**, because it is *multiplied* by a byte
of render state and the product is **clamped to `[0, 0x80]`**, stored into the emitted vertex record,
and **gates whether a quad is emitted at all** (`if (0 < value)`).

> **Both descriptions are of the same float and neither is refuted by the other.** It is decremented
> per character **and** used as a multiplier producing a per-quad quantity capped at 128. **A value
> that counts down and scales an output is a fade, a budget, or both — and the two readings together
> constrain it far better than either alone.**

**RESOLVED 2026-09-03, and it was never a disagreement.** A scan of the whole cluster for that
displacement:

| function | what it does with `+0x10E4` |
|---|---|
| the emitter | **read, `subss`, written back — decremented only.** No `mulss` in the body. |
| the shared text consumer | **`mulss` at two sites** — a render-state byte converted and multiplied by it, then compared |

> **One field, two roles: one function spends it, the other scales by it.** *"Scales an output"* and
> *"a remaining budget"* are **halves of one lifecycle**, and the apparent disagreement was **each
> reader seeing one function.** **Neither reading needed correcting; the field needed a second site.**

**And one of the coordinator's two offered facts was wrong.** The batch threshold is **`0x14`, not
`0x13`** — the counter is incremented and compared, and flushes when the count **reaches** 20:

```asm
002AA45B  add  rcx, 0x50                    ; the per-glyph record stride
002AA465  cmp  dword ptr [rdx + 0x10d4], 0x14
002AA478  call 0x2B4730                     ; flush
```

**Twenty records of 80 bytes — a 1,600-byte batch** — and **the same counter against the same `0x14`
appears in a second cluster function, so the batch rule is cluster-wide.** *The record stride is the
part the decompiler read could not give and the image read could.*

**The two-path finding was confirmed and closed a loop:** `0x00`–`0x0F` go to the shared consumer and
its return is tested — **the caller's view of the `char**` cursor and `int*` status characterised from
the callee side two blocks earlier, two descriptions derived independently, meeting exactly.** And the
lookahead constants `0x3E`, `0x70`, `0x40` are **the same three the markup parser uses**: *the markup
vocabulary is not per-function; the parser and the emitter agree by using the same constants.*

> **The duplicate produced a real independent corroboration — but it was luck, not design.** The fix
> is the announcement, not more care: **every assignment tonight was announced except the one that
> collided.** A clean single-variable result.

## Standing note: the engine's tunables are runtime-installed

**Three independent sightings tonight, in three unrelated subsystems:** the spawn activation range
*(documented as 43.0)*, the developer console's float sliders, and the four character-spacing globals.
**All sit in the zero-filled tail of `.data` and have no value in the shipped image.**

> **A modder dumping the executable to find any of them finds zeros.** The values are installed at
> runtime — by script, by a settings load, or by an initialiser — **so a static patch of the file
> changes nothing, and a static read of the file reports a tunable as absent when it is merely not yet
> written.**

**This is a rule, not a coincidence**, and it is the single most likely way for someone using this
database to reach a confident wrong conclusion from the binary alone.

**Two further observations from the unpublished read**, offered to the document's owner rather than
published separately: the emitter **flushes every 20 glyphs** — a counter at `context + 0x10D4`
compared against `0x13`, then a flush call and a reset — and the character loop has **two paths**, one
for bytes below `0x10` which goes through the shared text consumer **and returns a count of positions
to skip**, one for everything else which emits.

## "Not documented" and "not ours to document" are now different states — and my guess about which was wrong

**The distinction was raised as a gap in the SSE list and it is now a column.** The ledger already
knows: a function tagged into a **`Microsoft`, `ThirdParty` or `CompilerGenerated`** code domain is
library or compiler output. **5,253 functions carry such a tag; 14 of the 60 on the SSE list do.**

> **A bracketed row is not a documentation gap.** *We have not documented it* and *it is not ours to
> document* are different states — **the same distinction as *not measured* versus *contradicts*.**

### And the address that prompted it is not what I called it

I flagged `00864F30` as **probably CRT, on the strength of its address range** — `0x86xxxx`, up where
the runtime lives.

**The ledger's own tag is `GFZ.V121.CodeDomain.Phyre.SceneCamera.NativeQualified`.**

> **It is PhyreEngine scene-camera code, squarely in scope**, and this database has a whole category
> for the middleware it belongs to.

**I inferred provenance from an address range**, which is the banned-evidence species this project
opens its evidence standard with — *address adjacency is never semantic evidence* — applied to
provenance instead of to meaning. **The column now says so in its own output**, because the guess that
motivated it was wrong:

```
An UNbracketed row is engine or middleware code and IS in scope: address range is
not evidence of provenance, and this column exists because a guess from one was wrong.
```

**The instrument is better for the error**: a range guess would have excluded a scene-camera function
from a rendering-heavy database, and **the tag excludes exactly the fourteen that deserve it and none
that do not.**

## A frontier reaching zero is not a backlog reaching zero

**The leaned-on cut reports 0 — and what that means needs saying, because the output cannot say it.**

**Every one of the last four entries existed because opening a function cites its callees.** The list
stopped generating new ones only when the chain reached a function whose callees were already cited
elsewhere.

> **That is what "closed" means for an instrument like this: not that nothing is left unread in the
> binary, but that the citation graph has no remaining edge from a documented claim to an undocumented
> body.**
>
> **A frontier reaching zero and a backlog reaching zero look identical in the output and are
> completely different events.**

**23 originals, plus four the work itself created, plus the SSE list still at 60.** The database has
6,677 of 34,270 functions cited. **Nothing here is finished; one specific kind of debt is paid.**

## Four true numbers over four populations, for one function

A re-derivation of `001F0AD0` against a document that says **144 of 154 accesses**, where the SSE
census says **260**. **Both are right:**

| number | population |
|---:|---|
| **144** | `movss` **with a memory destination** |
| **13** | `movss` **loads** |
| **156** | distinct rip-relative **write targets** |
| **260** | **all seven SSE mnemonics, both directions** — the body also holds 96 `shufps`, 59 `movaps`, 44 `movups` |

> **Stated together so the next reader cannot pick one and call the others wrong.** This is the
> `movups`-versus-`movups+movss` population note again, at four levels instead of two, on a single
> function.

**And the substantive claims reproduced exactly:** 128 stores in `02CB8160`–`02CB835F`, **16 per
64-byte block**, with `02CB7FD0 + 0x190 = 02CB8160` confirming the offset framing.

## The read-but-uncited mode is a writing habit, not an instrument artefact

**Three functions, one author, one document — all read in detail, none cited internally.**

> **No list caused that and no list can detect it except by the absence.**

**Five re-derivations were run tonight to add anchors after the fact. All five confirmed the
documents; none produced a correction.**

> **That is a good result for the documents and a bad ratio for how the effort was spent.**
> **Per-claim anchors cost almost nothing while the body is open in front of you, and cost a full
> re-derivation afterwards.**

**Which places the fix earlier than cleanup** — it belongs in the writing, which is where step 3b of
the inbox protocol now puts it.

## The null test that comes second

```asm
0018A13C  movaps xmm0..xmm3, four constant rows   ; loaded FIRST
0018A158  test   rax, rax                         ; the source pointer
0018A15B  je     keep_the_constants
```

> **The four constant rows are loaded before the null test, not after it.** A source object with a null
> field yields them unchanged — **a missing matrix is a defined result, not a skipped write.**

**That is the difference between "no shadow" and "identity shadow", and only the ordering shows it.**
A sibling function on the same ranking adjusts a resolved pointer by `+8` when it is non-null and then
reads through it regardless — **whether the two share a convention is open, and the difference
matters.**

## A third ranking: touching the same thing, never compared

**Both existing lists score a single function** — bytes-per-line asks *how little did we say about this
one*, SSE density asks *what shape is this one*.

> **Almost every finding tonight came from reading a body, and almost every correction came from
> comparing two bodies.** Neither list can see a pair.

**So the third ranking is: a target reached by 2–6 functions where no single document cites two of
them.** Those functions have never been compared *in writing*, whatever else is known about them.

### Two censuses, because the motivating case was not a global

**The specification was wrong on first statement.** The `+0x10E4` case that motivated the whole idea is
**`[rdx + 0x10E4]` and `[r8 + 0x10E4]` — structure offsets, not globals.**

> **The ranking as first specified would not have caught the case that motivated it.**

**Two censuses now exist**, and they answer different questions:

| census | finds | size |
|---|---|---|
| rip-relative data references | **shared state** | 164,489 sites · 35,879 addresses · **11,694 touched by ≥2 functions** |
| `[reg + disp]`, `disp ≥ 0x400` | **shared structures** | 24,100 sites · 3,911 displacements · **1,135 touched by 2–6** |

**The struct census catches the motivating case and immediately extends it:** `0x10E4`'s touchers are
the two functions that were compared **plus a third neither reader had opened.**

**A caveat that travels with every row of the second list:** a displacement is only meaningful within
one structure type, and **the census cannot know whether `[rdx+0x10E4]` and `[r8+0x10E4]` are the same
field.** The `≥ 0x400` filter and the 2–6 band make it **a candidate generator, not a claim** — the
same status as bytes-per-line, not the same status as the vendor tag.

### The ranking key was wrong too, and the fix is the point

**First version ranked by fan-in**, and the top rows were addresses touched by five undocumented
functions — **coverage gaps, which the other two lists already own.**

> **Rank by how many touchers are *documented*.** A row where four of five are documented **and no
> document mentions two of them** is a genuine *comparison* gap. Fan-in breaks ties in the tight
> direction, because fewer touchers means the shared address is likelier to mean something.

**First two rows under the corrected key:**

```
+0x5A7E   5 touchers, 4 documented, 0 shared documents
          BattleStats_SetEquipmentSlotByMappedId · BattleSlotMap_ResolveMappedBattleSlot
          BattleMenu_BuildCommandEntryList · BattleSlotMap_InitializeMappingClasses

+0x06B5   3 touchers, 3 documented across 8 documents, 0 shared
          BattleUnit_ResetStateForReuse · BattleUnit_ExecuteDirectStateCommandOrder
          BattleUnit_SelectAndCommitNextAction
```

**The second is the sharper case: three heavily documented battle functions touching one offset, and
not one of eight documents mentions two of them together.**

**And a validation the tool passed by refusing a candidate.** A hand-built probe on `01DFE0B8` — the
frame-rate base, six readers — returned **nothing**, because two of its readers are already cited by
one document. **The filter did its job: that pair has been compared, and the list is for the pairs that
have not.**

## A defect in the census the pair ranking runs on, and the class it hid

**2,102 of the struct census's 24,100 rows were not struct offsets.** They are
`[reg + reg*scale + huge_disp]` where the base register **holds the image base** — the same idiom as
the console dispatch table. **The displacement is an RVA, not a field offset.**

> **A row this coordinator printed as `01E632B0` is really the global `01F832B0`** — wrong by
> `0x120000`, and in the wrong list. **Going to look at it would have found nothing there.**

**Verified rather than assumed:** of the 617 functions carrying such a row, **607 contain a proven
`lea reg, [rip] -> 00120000` in the same body**, and the other 10 are **flagged rather than dropped**.

### And the misfiled class is the most valuable part of the census

Its 1,209 distinct addresses against the rip-relative census: **186 already known, 1,023 new — 84.6 %.**

> **The rip census was blind to them because the base sits in a register rather than in `rip`.**
> Distinct globals **35,879 → 36,902** — and **these are exactly the addresses reached by dispatch
> tables and big authored data**, so *the class the first census missed is the class most likely to be
> a table worth naming.*

**It gets its own ranking rather than being merged**, for the same reason the shared-state and
shared-structure lists are kept apart.

**Two corrections inside that work, both caught before it was reported:** the class was first read as
*"rip-relative leaked in"* — **disassembling a site found a base register instead**; and the first
overlap figure was *"0 new out of 0 known"*, **because two files write addresses differently
(`0x`-prefixed versus bare hex) and the filter silently matched nothing.** The final numbers carry two
positive controls — the rip census reports its expected 35,879, and a known global is present in it.

> **A comparison between two censuses is a third instrument, and it needs its own control.**

## When the image-base trap can bite you, mechanically

**A session checked its own three image-base-relative tables against the coordinator's error rather
than recalling that it had been careful.** All three were right — **and the reason is structural, not
diligence.**

| table | entries | result |
|---|---:|---|
| console action dispatch | 282 | 282 in `.text`; **all 227 distinct targets decode as code** |
| row renderer dispatch | 294 | 294 in `.text`; **258 of 263 decode** |
| class registry | 32 | 32 in `.text`; **all 29 decode** |

### A jump table self-validates. A data table does not.

**All three are jump tables**: resolving the base and then *disassembling the targets* means a wrong
base produces garbage instructions **immediately** — the arms would not decode and the catalogue would
collapse on its first slice.

> **The verification was free and unavoidable, because reading the table meant executing its contents
> mentally.**

**The coordinator's was a 16-byte record table.** A wrong base **returns plausible bytes, and nothing
in a data dump can refuse to be data.** That asymmetry is the whole difference and it is not about
attention.

### And the mechanical rule for who is exposed

**Every *data* table that session resolved — a 13-entry presentation table, the powers of ten, a
294-entry label array — was reached through RIP-relative operands**, where the disassembler computes
the address and the base is encoded in the instruction. **The coordinator's case had the base in
`rsi`.**

> **`[rip + disp]` is checked by the decoder. `[reg + disp]` is checked by nobody.**
>
> **The exposure is not "am I being careful with RVAs" — it is "is the base in the instruction or in
> my head."**

**That is a rule you can apply before making the mistake**, which *"remember the image base"* is not.

### A census of an accessor's callers is not a census of a field's readers

**The console reads a field that has a dedicated accessor and does not call it**, so **a hook on the
accessor sees one reader and misses the other entirely.**

> Same species as one table reached through seven dispatcher arms: **two paths to one datum, and
> instrumenting either alone gives a complete-looking, wrong answer.**

## A fourth checker: named in prose, never cited by address

**The pair census reported an offset as still uncompared when a document had in fact compared both its
touchers at length.** The document named them **in pseudocode**, by name, and cited **no address inside
either.**

> **A document that names a function without citing it is invisible to every instrument here.**

**Adding two interior anchors cleared two frontier rows at once**, and the corpus-wide measurement
afterwards is large:

```
426 named-but-uncited mentions across 82 documents
```

**This is the read-but-uncited mode one step earlier.** The other checker asks *"was this body
opened?"*; this one asks *"did the document that talks about it say where it is?"*

**It reports suspicions and exits 0.** A passing reference — *"the same helper the other document
reads"* — is a reference, not a claim, and needs no anchor. **The signal is repetition:** a name
mentioned once is a mention; **twenty-seven distinct names in one document is a document whose subject
matter is unreachable from its own text.**

**And the failure it caught was in the document written to demonstrate the instrument that finds
it** — the same shape as the taxonomy entry that did not protect its author, and as the RVA trap walked
into one tick after being recorded. **Three times tonight, the thing a session had just written down
did not protect that session from it.**

## The all-ones convention has a reason, and the reason is representational

**Eight sightings across two sessions with no document owning it** — and the census that followed gives
**169 byte-stores and 87 compares across twelve subsystems.**

**But the reason came from one decoded function, not the count.** A pool record's `+0xD` and `+0xE` are
**one-byte prev and next indices** — `shl rax, 4 ; add rax, base` proves they are indices, not flags —
and `0xFF` is the null link.

> **That is why the sentinel is all-ones and not zero: index `0` is a real record.** In an index space
> over a pool, **zero is an occupant**, so the only free value is the other end of the range.
>
> **The convention is forced by the representation, not chosen for style.**

**And the test of that reading is the two-byte cases:** equipment slots and the reserved item id use
**`0xFFFF`, not `0xFF`** — which *"maximum of the space"* requires and *"the byte `0xFF` is special"*
does not.

**The counts and the meaning are kept apart, deliberately:** `mov byte [x], 0xFF` is equally how you
write a mask or a signed `−1`. **The strict test returns six fields; the 169 is published with its
limit stated** so a reader can see how much is uncharacterised.

> **Six read fields support the claim better than 256 counted ones.**

**The modding consequence is the payoff: zero is never "empty" in these fields.** Writing `0` into a
handle **does not clear it — it selects slot zero.**

**And the `+0x695`/`+0x696` pair arrived from both directions at once** — found here by reading the
cancel path, found there by censusing the idiom, **neither looking for the other.**

### The checker resolved names arbitrarily, and that was the same defect it was reporting

**Requested and fixed within the tick.** The first version mapped each name to **one** address, first
wins.

> **A name that maps to two addresses fails differently in every tool that assumes it maps to one.**

**In this corpus that is not hypothetical.** One seeding routine's name is carried by **both a 5-byte
thunk and its body**, and that single collision has now produced **two** distinct failures tonight:

- **a false "zero callers"**, from a query against the body while the call graph indexed the thunk;
- **a wrong anchor**, from a bulk fix writing the body's address onto the thunk's row.

**Now a name keeps every entry that carries it**, a mention counts as cited when **any** of them is
cited, and an ambiguous name **prints as `AMBIGUOUS` with all its addresses.** Current corpus:

```
375 mention(s) across 78 documents, 24 of them AMBIGUOUS
```

### And the fix to a checker's finding is output like any other

**A session anchored its three failing documents programmatically, precisely to avoid transcription
slips — and the blanket pass wrote addresses into tables whose address column already carried them,
including one row annotated with the wrong function's address.**

> **A fix applied to a checker's finding is output like any other and needs the same reading.** A bulk
> edit run to avoid one class of error introduced another; **only looking at the result separated
> them.**

**Seventeen redundant anchors stripped, the wrong one with them, caught by reading.** *Fourth
correction of the night from reading output rather than tabulating it.*

### The document whose purpose was reachability was the worst offender

**The class registry had 22 named-but-uncited functions** — built specifically so that four documents
each holding one row could see the whole relation, **and its own subject matter was unreachable from
its own text.**

> **A reference document that names without locating is a table of contents with no page numbers.**

### The checker can be satisfied dishonestly, and now says so in its own output

**`functions.tsv` will supply an address for every name on that list in one pass.** Pasting them in
clears the tool completely.

> **An address supplied from a name lookup is a transcription, not a citation.** A bulk anchor
> **asserts a reading nobody made** — and **the checker goes quiet while the document becomes *less*
> honest**, because a cited address reads as evidence that someone opened the body.
>
> **A checker measures what it can see; satisfying it is not the same as fixing what it points at.**

**The warning is printed by the tool itself**, above its own results, because the cheapest way to clear
this list is the one that damages the corpus.

**And the first document worked this way proves the difference is not theoretical.** Of twenty names,
**nine were anchored — the LCG's draw sites, its state setter, the area-init seed counter, the MT
thunk — all independently re-derived by the person anchoring them.** *Eleven were left listed*, with
the document recording **which nine, by whom, and why the eleven are absent.**

> **The gap stays visible instead of being papered over by the tool that found it.** The difference
> between the honest and the dishonest version of that edit **is one command wide.**

**Same hazard as the bulk anchor that broke a key-address table an hour earlier** — and the reason this
one was done by hand-picked list rather than by regex.

### And a correction to the routing that supports the reading rather than denting it

**The top three rows are not any current session's documents** — all three are dated **2026-09-02**,
the work this run built on.

> **The gap sits in documents written before the anchoring convention existed**, which is what the
> distribution suggested and is now checked rather than assumed.

### The ambiguity is mechanical, and the resolution depends on the question

**Fifty-seven names in the image are carried by more than one function**, classified by disassembling
every short entry and testing whether its first instruction is a `jmp` into its own group:

| | groups |
|---|---:|
| **thunk + body** | **53** (48 pairs, 3 triples, one four, one five) |
| thunk chain — a thunk whose target is a thunk | 1 |
| **genuinely distinct bodies** | **3** |

**All three genuine collisions are compiler artifacts** — a per-class deleting destructor, `malloc`,
and eight identical 47-byte throw helpers.

> **No engine function shares a name with a different engine function.** Every other collision is a
> name and its own thunk, **so the ambiguity is a property of the linker** and is resolvable without
> deciding which `Foo` was meant.

**And the resolution is not one answer:**

| question | resolve to | because |
|---|---|---|
| what calls this? | the **thunk**, or the union | **the body may have zero direct callers** |
| what does it do? | the **body** | the thunk is one instruction |
| what do I cite? | the **body**, unless the claim is about the thunk | an anchor should reach the code the claim describes |
| is it reachable? | the **union, followed transitively** | a chain exists |

> **A name is not an address in this corpus.** A tool that resolves one to the other silently **has
> answered a different question than the one it was asked** — which is how one collision produced a
> false *"zero callers"* and a wrong anchor within an hour of each other.

**The checker now prints body sizes beside every ambiguous address**, so the thunk is visible —
`00379CF0(5B) 00863D30(69B)` — **without the tool asserting a classification it did not perform.**

### 30 of 53: the caller-census exposure, measured

**A warning broadcast earlier — *every caller census here is exposed unless it follows thunks* — was
checkable, and checking it changed its status.** Counting direct callers of both entries across all 53
thunk/body groups:

> **30 bodies have ZERO direct callers while their thunk has some. 23 have callers on both.**

**The seeding routine was not a special case — it is one of thirty**, alongside a field-restore
requeue, **all four** input remask variants, and a save-buffer backup.

> **A zero from a caller census on one of those thirty is not evidence of anything.** It is the shape
> of the question — **and it is indistinguishable from a real absence**, which is what makes it
> dangerous rather than merely inconvenient.

**So any claim of the form "N callers" for a name in this class has to say which entry it counted.**
**The 24 ambiguous names this database's documents actually mention are exactly the population where
such a claim is suspect.**

> **A warning is a thing to remember; 30 of 53 is a thing to check against.**

**And the number is bounded rather than exact, by its author:** it is a direct-caller count from the
call graph, **so it inherits that graph's coverage** — a body reached only through a chained thunk or
an indirect call would show no callers either way. **The exposure is at least thirty; it cannot be
lower and could be higher.**

### The division that has held all night

**Printing `00379CF0(5B) 00863D30(69B)` and leaving the `jmp` test with whoever runs it** is the same
split as every instrument that has survived this run:

> **The tool reports what it measured; the reader supplies what it did not.**

**Five bytes against sixty-nine settles it at a glance** — without the checker asserting a
classification it never performed.

---

## The denominator was a true number about the wrong population (2026-09-03)

**`repos-2d` re-derived it and the census above was keyed on the wrong thing.** Dropping the
requirement that the thunk *share* its target's name:

| | functions |
|---|---:|
| have a short `jmp` thunk aimed at them | **750** |
| thunk **shares** the target's name | **58** |
| thunk carries a **different** name — invisible to a name-keyed census | **692** |

Of the 325 whose thunks carry callers: **140 targets return zero** from a body census and **185
silently undercount.**

> **The hazard is thunks, not duplicated names — and only 8 % of thunks share their target's name.**
> A census keyed on the name found **the name-shaped subset of a thunk-shaped problem.**

**`GameState_GetGil` was published as 19 callers. The body has 19; the thunk has 3 more with zero
overlap. The figure is 22.** Corrected in place by its author.

> **A zero looks like an absence; an undercount looks like a number.** The second is worse, and it
> is the larger population — **185 against 140.**

**So `24 ambiguous names` is not the review list; it is 8 % of it.** The 58 name-sharing cases have
been checked against the corpus. **The other 692 have not, and that is now written down rather than
assumed away.**

---

## A displacement census stops counting where a pointer starts (2026-09-03)

**CORRECTED, one tick after posting.** The first version of this section measured the shortfall with
`search_instructions` and attributed it to `struct_offset_refs_clean.tsv` — **two different
instruments, and the census is much the better of them.** The census holds 17 rows on this record
where the instruction search found 4. **The finding survives; every number in it was wrong.**

> **Measuring instrument A and reporting it as instrument B** is a mis-binding, and the reason it
> was caught is that the census was consulted directly for the *next* row rather than trusted.

**Measured against the census file itself** — `+0x2348`, `001E8070` / `001E8580`, written up in
[painting-a-line-of-text-into-a-render-target](../99-Unresolved-Residual/Glyph-Run-Rendering/painting-a-line-of-text-into-a-render-target.md):

| field | rows in the census | sites the document cites |
|---|---:|---:|
| `+0x2324` | 1 | 2 |
| `+0x233C` | **0** | 2 |
| `+0x2348` | 2 | 3 |
| `+0x2340` / `+0x2344` | 3, all writes | 5 |

**Two causes, and the second is the one worth keeping.**

**A base pointer** — `001E861C  lea r12, [r14 + 0x2348]`, everything below reads `[r12 - 0x24]`.
`001E8580` contributes **three** census rows for this record, all from above the `lea`.

**An offset folded into an index constant** — `add rax, 0xcd; imul rax, rax, 0x2c` puts `0x233C`
into the *index*, so `001E8408` stores to the field with no displacement in the instruction.
**`+0x233C` has zero rows in a census of 21,999.** Not undercounted: absent.

Two consequences, now written into `check_uncompared_pairs.py`:

- **Fan-in is not a measure of how busy a field is.** A row saying *two sites* may be a field with
  twenty.
- **"No writer found" is never a finding.** A writer holding the record pointer stores to
  `[reg + 0x25]` and is invisible by construction. `+0x2349` in that document has **no writer at any
  displacement in the image** and is plainly written by somebody — the document says so instead of
  reporting an absence.

**What the undercount does not do is invent rows.** Every site it reports is real, so a pair it
surfaces is a genuine pair. **It loses rows; it does not fabricate them** — which is the difference
between an instrument that is incomplete and one that cannot be trusted at all.

> **Both failures found tonight are the same failure at different keys: a census keyed on a name
> missed 692 thunks; a census keyed on a displacement missed every register-based access.** The
> lesson is not about names or displacements. **A census is blind to whatever its key is not.**

### And it inflates its own frontier: five rows that are one matrix

**Of the 13 rows left on the pair frontier, five are the same six functions** — `0016FC10`,
`001CCB20`, `001D1660`, `001E71B0`, `001E8580`, `001E9C80` — at `+0xE30`, `+0xE38`, `+0xE40`,
`+0xE48`, `+0xE50`.

**Those five displacements are one 4×4 float matrix.** `001E8580` reads it sixteen floats at a time
from `+0xE30` to `+0xE6C` (`001E8AE4`–`001E8BC8`), and twice as four `movups` (`001E902F`,
`001E9253`). **The census reports each 8-byte stride as an independent shared field, so one
structure produces five rows with an identical toucher set — 38 % of the remaining frontier.**

> **A frontier that lists a matrix five times is overstating how much work is left, in the one
> number a coordinator uses to decide what to read next.**

**And the widths disagree across the touchers**, which the census cannot show: `001CCB20` writes a
**byte** at `+0xE30` and `+0xE48` (`001CCDDD`, `001CCE01`) where `001E8580` reads **floats** and
`001E9C80` writes **dwords**. **A byte store into a float matrix element is a different object**,
so the six touchers are not all on one structure.

**The cheap fix is not a read.** Collapse rows with an identical toucher set at adjacent
displacements into one, and carry the access width so a byte/float disagreement is visible before
anybody opens a function. **Recorded, not built** — the frontier is still sound, it is only longer
than the work it represents.

---

## Two more blindnesses, both found by the same record (2026-09-03)

The `+0x2348` read kept producing them. Added to the four already listed (name key, displacement
key, encoding, size-as-a-value):

**A wide store credited to one field.** `001E73DA  mov word ptr [r14 + 0x2378], cx` writes **two**
byte fields. The census holds `001E71B0 / 0x2378 / write` and **no row for `+0x2379`** — same
instruction, same cycle, one field recorded. Likewise `001E73CA  mov word ptr [rax+0x28], 0x0101`
sets both mode bytes and is credited to neither.

**A constant materialised by arithmetic.** Three call sites pass the offscreen flag as
`lea r8d, [rdx - 0x43]` with `edx` already `0x44`. **The value is `1` and the instruction contains
no `1`.** A search for `mov r8d, 1` finds nothing at any of them.

**And the one that changed a conclusion: a search over DEFINED instructions cannot see undefined
bytes.** `search_instructions` reported `+0x2378` as having **one site image-wide**. The seven-byte
setter `001E9F30  mov byte ptr [rcx + 0x2378], dl; ret` writes it and was **not inside any function
Ghidra had defined**, so it was never scanned.

> **The tool reported 553,688 instructions scanned. It did not report what it declined to scan** —
> and an image-wide claim made from it is a claim about Ghidra's function table, not about the image.

**This is why "no writer found" is a rule and not a preference.** Twice in one read, a field with no
findable writer turned out to have one:

| field | first result | actually |
|---|---|---|
| `+0x2349` | no writer at any displacement | armed at `001E73CA`, re-armed every frame at `001E9920` |
| `+0x2378` | one site image-wide, a read | a setter exists in undefined code |

**And a conclusion was withdrawn on the strength of it.** A falsification row had reasoned that
`+0x2340` is written once at construction and never again — internally consistent, and wrong,
because the per-frame begin was one caller away. **The strangeness of the result was the tell that
the search was incomplete, not a finding about the engine.**

---

## A question can encode a wrong structural assumption (2026-09-03)

The glyph-run document listed as open: **"what `002AC5F0`, `002AAE40` and `002AF960` draw."**
`002AC5F0` draws nothing — it is the interpreter for the control codes embedded in game text — and
**`002AAE40` is one of its own seven callers.** The question named a function and its consumer as
peers, and asked what both of them did with a verb neither performs.

> **No answer to that question could have been right.** It is not an unanswered question; it is a
> malformed one, and it sat in a `DOCUMENTED` file looking exactly like the answerable kind.

**This is distinct from every defect class already listed here.** Absence, inflation, mis-binding and
fabrication are all properties of an *answer*. **This is a defect in the question**, and the
instruments cannot see it: `questions_closed` counts a malformed row exactly as it counts a good one,
so a corpus can improve its score by answering questions that should have been withdrawn.

**Two of tonight's own corrections are the same shape one level down:**

- *"What sets `+0x2378`?"* presumed a setter is reachable. Not established either way.
- *"Is `+0x2340` written once ever?"* presumed the search that found one writer had found them all.

> **A question inherits the assumptions of whoever wrote it, and then hides them** — because a
> question, unlike a claim, carries no evidence tier and gets no falsification row.

**No instrument is proposed for this.** The check that found it was a second reader opening the
function; that is the only thing that has worked on it so far.

---

## The qword-load inference (2026-09-03)

Published: *"`+0x1398` receives a pointer at three sites and the literal `0x3001` at a fourth"*,
reported as a contradiction in the engine. **Withdrawn within one tick.**

The three sites do `mov rcx, qword ptr [r14 + 0xb8]`. **A 64-bit load says the value is 64 bits wide
and nothing about what it means.** The word *pointer* was supplied by the reader.

> **Half of tonight's withdrawn claims came from a type asserted by the shape of an instruction** —
> a qword load read as a pointer here, a `lea` target read as a table base earlier, a `jmp` read as a
> tail call before that. **The instruction gives the width and the addressing mode. It does not give
> the type.**

**What made it recoverable was the same move both times:** the claim was published *as a
contradiction* rather than resolved into a story. A contradiction invites the next tick to attack it;
a smoothed reading does not.

---

## The question checker could not see a status after a one-character bold span (2026-09-03)

`check_questions.py` classified a register row by scanning the joined cells for
`**([^*]{2,}?)**` and taking the first bold span whose leading words match a status keyword.

**The regex cannot match a one-character bold span.** Having failed on `**Y**`, the scanner pairs
that span's **closing** `**` with the **next** span's **opening** `**` — and from there it reads
**the gaps between bold spans instead of the spans.** Every status after the first short span is
invisible.

| row | its question cell contains | old | correct |
|---|---|---|---|
| L-7 | `**Y**` | OPEN | **ANSWERED** |
| AO-4 | `**1**` | OPEN | **PARTIAL** |

**L-7's answer cell has begun `**ANSWERED 2026-09-02 …**` for a day.** The register was right; the
reader was wrong — and `questions_closed` reads this same table, so **the project score was
under-counting answered questions the whole time.**

**Fixed by reading the answer cell's own leading words**, which is what the function's docstring
already said it did. **The first version of the fix changed nothing**, because `cells` excludes the
id and the guard required more than two cells; a fix that changes no number is a fix that has not
run, and that is what made the guard get checked.

### The fix downgraded a row, and that mattered more than the two it promoted
