> **Under-claiming is the rarer failure and it is still a failure.** A document that hedges a proven
> claim teaches the next reader to distrust it, invites a re-derivation nobody needs, and — worst —
> **spends the reader's scepticism in the wrong place**, which is a finite budget. Calibrate downward
> *and* upward.

The corollary is the useful one: the reference should move from *"the corroboration this rests on"*
to *"a name for something already proven."* **Evidence tiers are about what carries the claim, not
about what is mentioned near it.**

### Co-occurrence of two common struct offsets is not a dataflow link

A scan for writers of `movementState + 0xA8` first asked *"does this function store to `[reg+0xA8]`
and also, somewhere, load `[reg+0xB8]`?"* — and returned **283 of 450 sites**.

`0xA8` and `0xB8` are ordinary offsets in a 64-bit struct; almost any large function touches both.
**The heuristic has no discriminating power and its output looked like a result.** Tracking the
register actually loaded from `+0xB8` and requiring the store to go through *that* register gives
**2**.

> **A filter that keeps 63 % of the corpus is not a filter.** Before believing a scan, ask what
> fraction it *rejects* — and run it against a known positive first.

### A constant's meaning at one site is not its meaning at every site

`0x10000000` appears in the PS2-GS frame builder twice. At one site it is written bare with a zero
second qword, and the document reasonably calls it a **terminator record**. At another — in
`0036D400`, wrapping the register batch — **the same tag carries a count**.

**So "terminator" is a reading of a *site*, not of the constant.** A document that names a constant
once and reuses the name everywhere will misread the second occurrence, and the misreading is
invisible because the name already looks established.

### An owner tag is a classification; census the callers to upgrade a claim built on one

`the-maths-the-game-runs-on.md` concluded *"the geometry path contains no SIMD at all"* because every
`sqrtps`/`rsqrtps` site in the image carries **owner = Audio**. That is a GFZ classification — a prior
session's claim — and if the twelve functions were a general vector library mis-tagged, the conclusion
would be wrong in the most damaging possible way: a true-sounding sentence about the wrong subsystem.

**The upgrade is cheap and it is not a re-reading of the same evidence.** Census the *callers*, which
the owner tag does not determine: **12 direct callers, all 12 Audio-owned**, none from Movement,
Presentation or anywhere else; the cluster's callees are Audio functions plus `__security_check_cookie`.

> **When a claim rests on a tag, look for a property the tag could not have produced.** Function
> ownership is assigned; who calls a function is not. The same move settled Tremor-versus-libvorbis
> (float against integer) and the `.ard` growth columns (clamp classes against struct names).

### A call-graph search cannot find a virtual method

Five of the twelve SIMD functions in the audio cluster have **zero direct callers**, including a
2,115-byte method holding **seven of the fourteen `sqrtps` in the executable**. They are virtual
methods reached only through vtables.

**Every prior pass over Audio walked call graphs, and this family was invisible to all of them.** It
became findable only when the recovered RTTI vtable map was applied — `B-3`'s 4,275
address-to-class-and-slot rows.

> **"No callers" from a call-graph query means "not called *directly*", and in a C++ engine that is
> the normal case for the most interesting functions.** A subsystem census built on the call graph
> systematically misses exactly the polymorphic core it most wants to find.

That was the stated value of the RTTI recovery when it was banked — *unlocking documents, not naming
functions* — and it took five days for a block to actually collect it.

### Four adjacent `_UNK_` symbols are one constant the decompiler could not name

`PolygonSound::slot34` computes every distance as `sqrt(Σ (P−Q)² · w)`, and Ghidra renders `w` as
**four separate symbols** — `_DAT_00D11570`, `_UNK_00D11574`, `_UNK_00D11578`, `_UNK_00D1157C` —
multiplied into four separate lanes.

**That reads exactly like per-axis weighting.** A weighted distance metric would have been a real and
interesting finding: it would let a designer make a sound attenuate faster vertically than
horizontally. The sentence was drafted before the bytes were read.

**The sixteen bytes are `(1.0, 1.0, 1.0, 0.0)`** — the W-lane kill mask, at the *same address* the
maths library document already documents for the masked 3-component dot product. It is an ordinary
3-D Euclidean distance computed 4-wide.

> **The rendering manufactures the appearance of four independent parameters** by giving each lane of
> one vector constant its own symbol. **The story would have survived every checker in the kit** —
> it cites a real address, it is internally consistent, and nothing cross-references a constant's
> *value*.

**Whenever a decompiled expression multiplies consecutive `_UNK_` symbols four bytes apart, read the
bytes.** It is one aligned constant, and the number of "parameters" is an artefact of the display.

### Two false findings in one day, both from decompiler *renderings* rather than decompiler errors

| rendering | what it looked like | what it was |
|---|---|---|
| `target_bkbi` typed struct | four record fields named Attack / MagickResist / two guard rates | the **same slots** the other branch calls Strength / Magick / Vitality / Speed — one record named two ways |
| four lane symbols at `00D11570` | per-axis distance weights | one `(1,1,1,0)` mask |

**Neither was a decompiler bug.** In both cases the tool rendered something true in a form that
suggested something false, and **no checker in the kit inspects either** — one is a name, the other
is a constant's value. **The raw bytes settled both in a single step.**

### Counting call edges and calling them callers — and the coincidence that hides it

A caller census reported *"the twelve functions have **12 direct callers**, all Audio-owned."* There
are **nine distinct callers**; twelve is the number of call **edges**.

> **The edge count happened to equal the function count**, which is exactly what made it invisible.
> Twelve functions, twelve callers reads as a tidy one-to-one and invites no second look.

**And four of the nine were members of the twelve** — the cluster vouching for itself. Removing
self-references leaves **five** external supports. The conclusion survived; the support was inflated
fourfold.

**Two rules.** *Deduplicate before reporting a census, and say which you counted* — "sites",
"edges" and "distinct functions" are three different numbers and a document that says "callers" has
picked one silently. *And when a set is asked to vouch for itself, exclude its own members first* —
internal callers are evidence about cohesion, never about independence.

The document's own neighbouring sentence — *"the five heavy methods have no direct callers"* — was in
tension with "12 callers" on the same page. **An internal contradiction one paragraph apart survived
authorship and a gate run**, because nothing checks a document against itself for arithmetic.

### A table that partitions part of a set reads as partitioning all of it

The same document's class table accounted for **4 of 12** SIMD-bearing functions and was laid out as
a complete taxonomy. The other eight carry SIMD and no class attribution.

**Nothing in the table was false.** The defect is that a reader counting rows would conclude the
family is fully attributed. **State the denominator inside the table, or state what it excludes** —
a taxonomy is a claim about coverage, not only about the rows present.

### "I could not find X" is an absence claim about the database

Third instance this session. A document recorded *"the caller that passes `ecx` was never found"* and
published the conclusion as an inference from constants. **The caller exists, there is exactly one,
and it was in the call graph** — and the configuration field it reads was **already documented in
another category of this database.**

The three: the registry census (**39 of 40** slots already cited), the shipped debug menu (documented
since 2026-09-03), and this caller.

> **The scanner rule has an exact twin.** *A zero from an instrument is a claim about the instrument*
> — and **"I could not find it" is a claim about the search, not about the engine.** Both need a
> positive control; for the database, that control is a grep.

**And the habit pays out rather than merely preventing errors:** the auditor grepped for `+0x48`
**before** calling it unknown, which is what turned a dead end into a closed read chain ending at a
named `GameSetting.ini` field.

### Never bring a provenance stamp "to current" — the tidying that destroys the trail

A falsification pass flagged a document's `verified_against: GN158` as a defect because the certified
parent is GN160, **and updated it.** That was wrong on two counts, and it was caught by the peer
auditing the same document.

**The stamp records what the document was verified against.** It was last reviewed on 2026-09-02 when
GN158 was current — the frontmatter working as designed. **What is stale is the review, not the
stamp**, and the fix for a stale review is a re-verification that then updates it.

**And a partial pass cannot update a whole-document stamp at all.** The pass examined four claim
groups and explicitly did not examine six sections; re-stamping claimed coverage it did not have.

> **This is the failure mode the rule exists for, and it is dangerous precisely because it does not
> feel like a claim.** *"Bring the stamp to current"* is a one-line change that looks like
> housekeeping, passes every gate in the kit, and **erases the record of which program state the
> document's evidence was actually gathered against.** Nothing downstream can detect it.

**It was made by the session that had just flagged the stamp as suspicious** — noticing that a field
is out of date and *correcting the field* is the natural next motion, and it is exactly backwards.

### Two pass counters with two names and two values

`spawn-placement-height-and-authored-tunables.md` carries **`audit.passes: 0`** and
**`falsification_passes: 1`** simultaneously — the latter from a real pass by `repos-9e` on
2026-09-03 recording *"0 overturned, 11 claim groups survived, 2 refinements."*

**Both readings are supported by the file, and it already caused a misreading:** the document was
handed to an auditor as *"`passes: 0`, never falsified"* when it had had a full pass. **The reader
takes whichever field they see first.**

**And `progress.py` counts `falsification_passes`** — which is why completing a new pass on this
document moved `audit_coverage` by zero. Documents whose own text says *"adversarial work performed
(not yet a full falsification pass)"* are already being scored as audited.

> **Decide which counter is canonical.** Until then `audit_coverage` is measuring one field while
> sessions triage on the other, and a document can be simultaneously *"owing an audit"* and *"already
> counted"*.

### The grep rule protects the person being audited, not only the auditor

An auditor grepped the document for `009146EC` **before** describing the constants as missing from
it. Without that they would have filed *"the author missed the constants"* — false, and insulting.
What they filed instead was *"the author found them, and §1 contradicts §3."*

**Same evidence, opposite conclusion about a colleague's work.**

> The rule was written to stop absence claims about the *engine*. It turns out to matter just as much
> for absence claims about a *person's research* — and there the cost of getting it wrong is not only
> a wrong finding.

### `audit_coverage` checks that a pass stamp *exists*, never what it *says*

**Two sessions censused this metric independently on 2026-09-07 and found two different things. Both
are real and they compose.**

#### What the scorer actually does — and a correction to this session's first reading

`progress.py`:

```python
stamped = (passes > 0 and last_pass_state and last_pass_state != "null")
if stamped or (falsification_passes > 0 and has_falsification_section):
    audited += 1
```

**A bare counter earns nothing in either branch.** `xiionline-8d` established this by reading the
code; **this session first inferred the rule from the surrounding comments and got it wrong**,
claiming the metric was "96 % driven by `passes`". It is driven by `passes` **plus a state stamp**,
and the comments say the stamp requirement was added *because* bare counters were the original
defect.

> **The two readings produced the same number — 163 — for different reasons**, because **no document
> in the corpus has `passes > 0` without a state stamp.** A wrong model of an instrument can
> reproduce its output exactly when the corpus does not separate the models. **Agreement with the
> tool is not evidence that you have understood the tool.**

#### The finding that survives, and it is about the guard's contents

The guard requires `last_pass_state` to be **present and non-null**. **It does not read what the
state says.** Of the 163 documents scored audited:

| `last_pass_state` | count | is it an attack? |
|---|---:|---|
| `first-read-repos-55` | 16 | **no — a first read** |
| `reverification-against-current-parent` | 12 | **no — a provenance check** |
| `console-join-repos-55` | 9 | **no — a work description** |
| `pair-frontier-repos-55` | 7 | **no** |
| `residual-walk-repos-55` | 4 | **no** |

> ⚠ **CORRECTED — the figure is 12, not 48.** This read the state *string* and inferred that no
> attack happened **without checking the body**. `xiionline-8d` checked: **39 of the 51 non-attack
> stamps carry a written `## Falsification pass` section anyway** — `the-basis-column-helpers.md` is
> stamped `first-read-repos-55` and has one; so does `what-the-engine-fixes-at-boot.md`; so do four
> console arm-catalogue slices. **The string is a poor label, not missing work.**
>
> **Genuinely scored-but-never-attacked: 12 documents, 6.0 %** — and *all twelve are one cohort*,
> every one stamped `reverification-against-current-parent`. One session's re-verification sweep,
> written into `last_pass_state` as though it were a pass.

So `audit_coverage` measures *"carries a stamped pass of some kind"*, not *"survived a named attack"*
as its own definition states. The stamp requirement fixed the **bare-counter** hole and left the
**meaningless-stamp** hole open, and the second is larger than the first ever was.

**The affected documents are load-bearing, which is why it matters more than a percentage:**
`where-a-stat-number-comes-from.md`, `the-moment-an-action-is-committed.md`, `rng-streams.md`, five
Weather documents, three of the Rewards chain. **Sessions triage on this number** — a document scored
audited is not offered to a cold auditor.

#### A latent hazard in the other branch, found by `xiionline-8d`

The section guard's regex `^#{2,3} .*[Ff]alsification pass` **matches a heading that denies one** —
`## 10. Adversarial work performed (not yet a full falsification pass)`. Measured across the corpus:
**zero** documents currently have a negating heading as their only one, so the hazard is **latent,
not firing** — but the spawn document had exactly that shape earlier the same evening, and is clean
now only because a `passes: 1` stamp was added while it was being audited. **One frontmatter edit
from firing.**

Their fix changes **no** document's score against the current corpus, which is the right property
for a guard — it closes the hole without moving the number, so the argument stays about the hole:

```python
heads = re.findall(r"^#{2,3} .*[Ff]alsification pass.*", t, re.M)
has_section = any(not re.search(r"not yet|not a full|owes|pending|no falsification", h, re.I)
                  for h in heads)
```

#### Do not merge the two counters

`audit.passes` + `last_pass_state` records *checked against a named program state*.
`falsification_passes` + `last_falsification_by` records *someone tried to destroy it, and who*.
The spawn document carries both, saying different things about **two different events** — a GN160
re-verification today and a GN158 attack by `repos-9e` on 09-03, with a recorded result.

> **Collapsing them erases the distinction between re-verified and attacked, which is the
> distinction Phase D rests on.** And a mass frontmatter edit across 161 documents is precisely the
> class of harm the provenance-stamp incident demonstrated an hour earlier.

**Nothing was applied.** Changing a scoring rule reprices every historical figure in `COVERAGE.md`
and `AUDIT-LOG.md`; that is a decision about the project's own measure, not a fix to a document.
Recorded with the measurements so it can be decided with the numbers in view.

### A hooked function is not a hooked mechanism when the compiler inlined it

`rng-streams.md` documents `RNG_SharedGameplayLCG_NextU32` as *the* shared gameplay draw. The
multiplier `0x41C64E6D` appears in **8 functions across 24 sites** — the complete three-step sequence
in each, every copy reading and writing the same state word — and **only one of the eight is that
function.** The other seven are `EnemyChain_*` and `Loot_Roll*`.

> **Hooking the named function intercepts one draw in eight**, and the seven it misses are loot and
> the enemy chain. A synchronisation mod built on it would appear to work and would desynchronise
> exactly what players notice.

**Before naming a function as a system's entry point, census its defining constant.** A named
function is a *source-level* boundary; the compiler is under no obligation to preserve it, and a hot
single-call-site helper is the first thing it dissolves. **The durable intercept is the state, not
the call.**

### A state string is a comment about a pass

A census classified documents as never-attacked by reading `last_pass_state` and finding it named
something other than an attack. **39 of 51 carried a written falsification section anyway.** The real
figure was **12, not 48** — a fourfold overclaim published into two index files before a peer checked
it.

> **`last_pass_state` is prose about a pass in exactly the way a code comment is prose about a
> function.** This project's first rule is that source beats prose, and **the body of the document is
> the source here.** Read the section, not the label.

**And the scale was the damage, not the number.** *"A quarter of the database is unattacked"* is a
project-level alarm that reprices every audit figure and dispatches sessions at 48 documents; *"one
re-verification cohort of 12"* is an afternoon. **A wrong magnitude prescribes a different action,
and that is what a wrong magnitude costs.**

### A constant can be absent from the image while the algorithm it defines is exactly present

`0x9D2C5680` and `0xEFC60000` — the two most recognisable constants in MT19937 — occur **zero times
in the entire 32 MB image**, as immediates or as data. **The zero is real and was verified with a
control**: `0x9908B0DF` occurs 10 times, `0x6C078965` 10, `0x41C64E6D` 24.

**And the tempering is exact.** The compiler rewrote `(y << s) & M` as `(y & (M >> s)) << s` — legal,
because the bits the mask clears are the bits the shift discards — which replaces both famous
constants with unrecognisable ones:

```
0x9D2C5680 >> 7  = 0x013A58AD    the body holds 0xFF3A58AD, and (0xFF3A58AD & 0x01FFFFFF) = 0x013A58AD
0xEFC60000 >> 15 = 0x0001DF8C    the body holds 0xFFFFDF8C, and (0xFFFFDF8C & 0x0001FFFF) = 0x0001DF8C
```

Both forms were **executed over 200,000 random 32-bit inputs: zero mismatches.**

> **Stopping at the census would have published *"the game's Mersenne Twister omits the tempering
> step"*** — false, and ruinous for anyone reproducing the stream for a deterministic client.
> **The absence is real at the byte level and false at the semantic level, and only reading the body
> separates the two.**

**A constant search finds a *spelling*, not an algorithm.** Strength reduction, constant folding and
mask migration all preserve behaviour while destroying the literal — and the more famous the
constant, the more confident the wrong conclusion feels. **Verify an algorithm by executing both
forms, not by matching its signature.**

### Two streams in one engine with opposite hookability

| stream | defining constant | functions | inlined? | hookable at a function? |
|---|---|---:|---|---|
| shared gameplay LCG | `0x41C64E6D` | **8** | **yes**, 7 inline copies | **no** — 1 draw in 8 |
| MT19937 | `0x9908B0DF` | **2** | no | **yes** |

**Nothing in either name says which.** `RNG_SharedGameplayLCG_NextU32` and `RNG_MT19937_NextU32` read
as peers and behave oppositely under a hook. **Census the defining constant before treating any
named function as a system's entry point** — and where the count exceeds one, the state word is the
only durable intercept.

### An instrument reported as a failure rather than a result

The same census applied to the presentation bit-feedback PRNG returned **39 functions and 1,076
sites** for `0x1FC00000`. That is a common bit pattern, not a signature: **the selector has no
discriminating power and its output is not evidence about anything.**

Recorded as `CQ-5` **unanswered**, rather than as a finding about 39 functions. *The same scan gave a
clean answer for two streams and no answer for a third, and the difference is a property of the
constants, not of the streams.*

### The paired-row defect, found three times and now fixed rather than recorded a fourth

`check_consistency.py` pairs every address in a table row with every name in that row, so a row
listing a *pair* manufactures a disagreement. Recorded on 2026-09-07 when `00902FD0` was diagnosed as
a false positive — and then **committed by the session that recorded it**, in a new RNG table pairing
two Mersenne instances.

Three instances of one shape:

```
| `00902FB0` / `00902FD0` | `BegineSceneObj` / `EndSceneObj` vtables |     <- 17-Presentation
| `00866B90` / `00866D60` | `RNG_MT19937_TwistState` / `SeedState`   |     <- 21-RNG
| gameplay | `…TwistState` `00866B90` | `…SeedState` `00866D60` | …   |     <- written the same day
```

**All three documents were factually correct.** The rows read well, pair naturally, and defeat the
only checker that guards naming. **All three are now split one-address-one-name-one-row, and
`check_consistency` reports clean for the first time this session** — the `00902FD0` finding had been
carried as a known false positive for hours.

> **Recording a writing rule does not enforce it, and the author of the rule is not exempt.** The
> defect was documented in this file and reproduced within the same day by the same session. **When a
> defect recurs three times, fix the instances rather than sharpen the description.**

A checker that cannot be satisfied gets reclassified as noise — *"the one remaining disagreement is
the known false positive"* was said several times today, and each repetition made the next real
disagreement easier to miss. **A gate at permanent 1 is a gate at 0.**

### A reciprocal search finds only the multiply form

Verifying `scale = Strength * (Level + Speed) / 218 + 1`, a census reported **`1/218` occurring zero
times in the image** against **49** occurrences of `0.00390625`. Both facts are true and neither is a
defect.

> **The Bow divides and the Crossbow multiplies.** `1/256` is a power of two and exactly
> representable, so `x / 256` → `x * 0.00390625` changes no bit and the compiler is free to do it.
> **`1/218` is not exactly representable**, so under `/fp:precise` the division must remain a
> division — and the binary holds `divss xmm0, 218.0`, with the divisor as a plain `218.0` constant.

**Searching for a reciprocal presumes the strength reduction happened.** Where it is forbidden, the
constant is present in the form the search did not look for. **Search for both the divisor and its
reciprocal, or read the body.**

**Second block running in which an absent constant was a compiler decision rather than a missing
operation — and the two resolve in opposite directions**, which is what makes the pair instructive:

| case | the constant | what happened |
|---|---|---|
| MT19937 tempering | `0x9D2C5680`, `0xEFC60000` | **transformation happened** — masks migrated across the shift, constants gone |
| Bow scale | `1/218` | **transformation forbidden** — `/fp:precise` kept the division, constant present as `218.0` |

**In one case the operation was there and the constant was not; in the other the constant was there
and the search was wrong about its form.** Both look identical from a census: a zero.

### Three documents agreeing without citing each other

This block's pass produced a third independent corroboration between documents written apart:

- **`/fp:precise`** — inferred by the maths document from what the compiler *refused to inline*
  (4 `sqrtss` against 566 `sqrtf` calls), and here from what it *refused to strength-reduce*
  (`divss` by 218.0 where a reciprocal multiply would have been faster).
- **The variance guard** — `AttackPower * 0.125` compared against `0.01`, then
  `rand() % (int)(ATK * 12.5) / 100` — recorded independently in
  `attack-defense-preparation-and-stat-scaling.md` and reproduced instruction-for-instruction here.

> **Corroboration that nobody arranged is the strongest evidence this database produces**, and it
> only becomes visible when a document is attacked rather than re-verified. Three of these have now
> surfaced in one day — the elemental-affinity byte offsets, the `.ard` spine, and this.

---

## 2026-09-07 — `xiionline-61` — the MP economy, and the first movement in `registry_explained`

**Block: registry slot 22 (`0x02EBF0B8`), explained rather than declared.** `registry_explained` had
been flat at **9 of 63 all session** — the one measurable that never moved. It is now **10 of 63**.
The criterion is a `registry_slots:` frontmatter list, which is a *declaration* and not a proof, so
the constraint set at the start of the block was: **read every consumer first, declare last.**

New document: [`../10-Status-Periodic-Terminal/where-mp-comes-from.md`](../10-Status-Periodic-Terminal/where-mp-comes-from.md).
Documents 202 → 203.

### What was learned

**MP has four income paths and they are all one table.** Walking, being hit, hitting, and killing.
Rows **1–19** of slot 22 are the damage→factor ladder; rows **20–24** of the *same slot* are the
walk-recovery ladder. Nobody had noticed the second region.

- **A tick of walk-recovery is worth exactly 1 MP** — `003110C3` passes the step function's boolean
  return straight in as the delta, with flags `0x90`.
- **Flags `0x90` vs `0x10` is why walking is silent and being hit is not.** Both suppress `003283D0`;
  only walking also suppresses `0028F1F0`.
- **Spending MP throws away the part-charged next point** — `ModifyPrimaryResource` clears the `+0x70`
  accumulator on any decrease. That is what makes the float an MP accumulator and not a generic scalar.
- **Effect 39 pins MP to zero** by zeroing the capacity — a floor and a ceiling at the same number.
- **Neither block reads the table's authored row count.** Both bounds are compiled in (19 and 5), so
  rows added to slot 22 are unreachable. The recovery loop's `i < 5.0` escape is **dead code** given
  the shipped `4.0` and `5.0` constants.

### Three cross-document joins, none of which either side knew about

1. **`PROPOSED-repos2d-2` is half answered.** The navimapdata document records field `flags` bits
   12–13 as an open 2-bit selector. **Values 2 and 3 — and only those — triple MP gain from damage and
   divide the walk-recovery interval by 3.0.** For the MP economy the field is the one-bit test `>= 2`,
   true in **173 of 1,154 fields**. Its `AiOrder_EvaluateTargetSelector` consumer is unread, so **the
   register row and the checkbox both stay open.**
2. **The kill flag is the field document's own out-of-play predicate.** `Battle_ProcessActionTarget`
   computes `post_damage_mode` as `outOfPlay(target) && FieldData_GetActiveFieldRecordBit15() == 0` —
   the `0x80000003` / bit-15 pair, reused as "did this hit fell it?"
3. **Five new entries for the two-layer namespace map**, which had three. Two of them are in bytes
   that map had not reached at all — byte 4 (id 39) and byte 10 (id 86).

### ⚠ A wrong citation for a right conclusion, caught before publication

The draft asserted *"`+0x28` is MP; the stat document identifies it by its 999 clamp."* **The stat
document says `+0x28` is Vitality.** Its `+0x24` row is MP.

The conclusion survived; the reasoning did not. Those are **`.ard` growth/base column** offsets, and
the stat rebuild writes its **output block at `record + 0x24`** — the two structures are offset by
four. `.ard` `+0x26` Strength lands at record `+0x2A`, which is the byte the Bow formula reads, so
`.ard` `+0x24` MP lands at record `+0x28`, `s16` in both. `BattleStats_ModifyPrimaryResource` then
clamps `+0x4C` against `+0x28`, and the periodic step keys its ladder on `+0x4C` — two independent
confirmations.

> **Citing a table is not the same as citing the right table.** The wrong one was four bytes and one
> structure away, named a real stat, and would have read as authoritative to anyone who did not open
> it. **Open the document you are citing, every time — a remembered offset is a claim, not a source.**

### An instrument check that was worth its cost

The GFZ comment on `00300CE0` states *"modifies BKBI+0x4C with native clamping against BKBI+0x28
capacity."* That is a prior session's claim, and the standing rule is never to defer to one by
provenance. It was checked against the disassembly and **it is correct** — but the same read produced
four things the comment does not contain: the zero-pinning effect, the flag-bit table, the
post-publisher eligibility re-check on decreases, and the accumulator reset. **A correct annotation
is still not a reading.**

### Caster and target settled without the ledger

The two gain functions have no named parameters in the decompiler output, and the labels "Target" and
"Caster" come from ledger symbols. `Battle_ProcessActionTarget` settles it independently: **both calls
take the same `(caster, target)` pair, and each function's result is credited back to the participant
its name claims.** The ledger is right — and it is right on evidence now, not on its own say-so.

### Not examined

The shipped contents of slot 22 (`CS-2` — a proven layout over unread data), the spend side
(`00309110`, `CS-5`), `BattleStats_ModifySecondaryResource` (`CS-3`), the two publishers (`CS-4`), and
the `0x40`-stride per-combatant state arrays (`CS-6`). **The MP income is documented; the MP outgoings
are not**, and the document says so.

### Gates

`check_links` (10,221 links, all resolve), `check_names`, `check_consistency` (0 disagreements, 0
frontmatter gaps, 0 duplicate keys), `check_questions` (table and checklists agree), `check_formats`,
`check_provenance` — all green after the edit.

## 2026-09-07 — `xiionline-61` — ⚠ THE WORKING PROJECT WAS TWO CHECKPOINTS BEHIND ALL SESSION

**`Verify-GfzCheckpoint` failed at the start of this block, and it should have been run five days
ago.** The finding is worth more than the block it interrupted.

### What was wrong

`work/headless/GFZ_HEADLESS`, imported **2026-09-02 01:08** and never re-imported, reports:

```
explicit=38003  unresolved=158  pct=99.58596
snapshot 46f02e26104e44215f8793700fe95fc243394d6b966b366e74f16110627bbbb2
residual c5d4b7f7fb1ffbf0c3f54c671bb0af02b0513901af634c5ca755333ce3d338ff
```

Those three values **match the `GN158` lineage entry in `GfzEnv.ps1` byte for byte.** The certified
parent has been **GN160** since 2026-09-03.

### Why nothing caught it

`GfzEnv.ps1` **was** updated to GN160 on schedule — the non-negotiable was obeyed. **The project was
not**, and the two are independent. Every wrapper auto-discovers the program name *from the project*,
so `-Mode Process` ran without complaint and printed
`…GM160_RECOVERY_TRANSFER_99P58_2026-08-31`, which reads like a parent name and is not the current
one.

> **A rule that guards one artifact does not guard the other.** "Update the parent block after every
> checkpoint" and "re-import the working project after every checkpoint" are two obligations, and only
> the first is written down. `Verify-GfzCheckpoint` is the only detector, it is run at session start,
> and **a project that goes stale between sessions is used unverified until someone runs it again.**

### The blast radius, measured rather than estimated

**Bounded and small.** The two checkpoints differ by exactly **19 functions**, all classified out of
the residual (GN159 four, GN160 fifteen — batch `R55TEXT`). Enumerated by diffing the certified
residual lists:

```
001E8070 001E8580 00245170 002455A0 00245E60 002AAE40 002B8EE0 002BCB00 002DA2D0 002DA7C0
002DBDD0 002DC290 002DC7F0 00364530 003649A0 00366110 00392A10 00392CE0 0053E0B0
```

Every one is cited **only** under `99-Unresolved-Residual/` (the glyph / widget / nine-patch / icon
cluster), plus `002AAE40` in two `16-UI/Text-And-Number-Rendering/` documents, plus the index files.
**None of the thirteen documents carrying `last_reviewed: 2026-09-07` cites any of them.**

**And the binary never changed.** The embedded authorized EXE is `73461d08…` at both checkpoints, so
every instruction, constant, offset and formula read this session is unaffected. What the stale
project supplied was **names, sizes and the decompiler's C rendering** — nothing else.

### What was actually wrong in the work, and what was not

The certified GN160 parent was fresh-imported to `work/headless_gn160/GFZ_GN160` and
**`Verify-GfzCheckpoint` passed on all four axes**. All twenty functions named by
`where-mp-comes-from.md` were re-decompiled against it.

**Every name reproduced. Every size reproduced but one.**
`BattleStats_ModifySecondaryResource` is `0xCF` = **207 bytes**; the document said **288**.

> **288 was the length of the disassembly window I chose to look at that function with** — a tool
> argument that walked into a table as though it were a measurement. Nothing flagged it: it is the
> right order of magnitude, it sits in a column of real sizes, and the size of a function I had
> explicitly declined to read is exactly the number nobody re-checks. **A figure that entered as an
> argument and left as a fact.**

### The stamp problem, and the shape of the honest failure

Thirteen documents carry `last_reviewed: 2026-09-07` with `snapshot_sha256: b57264ba…` (GN160). The
work behind them ran on GN158. `where-mp-comes-from.md` has now **earned** that stamp by
re-derivation, recorded in its own §8. **The other twelve have not**, and are filed as `CT-1`.

The contrast that makes the lesson concrete sits one directory away. **`the-unresolved-158.md` is
stamped GN158 — honestly, because that is what it was written against — and is stale by nineteen
functions. The twelve are stamped GN160, look current, and are unverified.**

> **An honest stale stamp is a better failure than a current-looking unverified one.** The first tells
> you what to re-check; the second tells you there is nothing to re-check. This is the same defect
> `xiionline-8d` caught earlier in this session from the opposite direction — *"'bring the stamp to
> current' is a one-line change that looks like tidying and quietly destroys the audit trail"* — and
> the general form is now visible: **the stamp must record the state the work actually ran against,
> whether that is flattering or not.**

A supersession notice was added to `the-unresolved-158.md` **without touching its
`verified_against` block**, and a warning added to `Database/Tools/README.md`.

### Not done

No successor census of the GN160 residual of 139. No re-derivation of the other twelve documents.
The Phyre block that `coverage_balance` selected (`22-Middleware-PhyreEngine`, debt 615, the largest
in the database) was **not started** — this displaced it.

### `CT-4` answered the same day — a size census over the whole database

**Filing it was not enough when it could be run.** Every `<address> … <N> B` claim outside `_index/`
was checked against the GN160 function graph (`work/graph/functions.tsv`, the pack-time table).

**1,093 readable size claims. 1,092 of them stand. Three corrections, one known ledger defect.**

| document | address | was | is | how settled |
|---|---|---:|---:|---|
| `scriptvm-native-dispatch-table.md` | `003DC240` | 36 B | **39 B** | body runs to the `ret` at `003DC266` |
| `how-a-line-of-text-becomes-glyph-quads.md` | `00364980` | ~24 B | **28 B** | `ret` at `0036499B` |
| `how-a-line-of-text-becomes-glyph-quads.md` | `00203170` | ~60 B | **44 B** | `ret` at `0020319B` |

All three were confirmed **from the authorized EXE** — the terminating `ret` before the `int3`
padding — not from the graph, so the graph is corroboration rather than authority. The `003DC240`
case was a real internal disagreement: the dispatch-table document said 36 in prose while the
verb-dispatch document's table said 39. **39 is right.**

**`00350320` is not a finding — it is the ledger being wrong, and three documents already say so.**
The ledger gives it 56 bytes; the real function is 11, ending in a tail jump. The census flags it
three times because it compares *against* the ledger. **A mismatch means one of the two is wrong, and
which one is a question the binary answers, not the census.**

### ⚠ Two instrument failures on the way, both mine, both self-inflicted

**First attempt returned 146 findings — essentially all false.** The rule was "any size within 120
characters after any address", which cheerfully matched offsets (`+116 bytes in`), the size belonging
to a *different* column of a multi-address table row, record sizes (`line records (32 bytes each)`),
memset lengths (`zero 29,712 bytes`), and corpus totals (`283,340,853 B`).

**Second attempt returned 133 — and roughly twenty of those were manufactured by my own regex.**
`([0-9]{1,4})\s*B` reads `13,753 B` as **753 B** and then reports the document as wrong by 13,000.
Every large function in the console documents lit up.

> **Both failures produced findings, not errors** — no exception, no zero, no obviously broken output.
> A scanner that returns 146 confident rows looks like a productive instrument, and the only thing
> separating that from a productive instrument is reading the rows. **The false-positive rate was
> visible in the first three lines of output; the manufactured-error rate was visible only after
> asking why every finding was short by exactly a thousands digit.**

The final rule is deliberately narrow — exactly one address and one size on the line, address first,
no `+` prefix, commas honoured — and it declares **197 lines unreadable rather than guessing at
them.** *An instrument that reports what it cannot read is worth more than one that reports a number
for everything.*

**What this does not cover:** the 197 unreadable lines, anything in `_index/`, sizes stated without
the word "B"/"bytes", and — the case that started this — a size that is wrong in *both* the document
and the ledger. `check_consistency` remains blind to all of it: it flags an address described two
different ways, and a figure stated once agrees with itself.

## 2026-09-07 — `xiionline-61` — the Phyre cluster header, and the first Phyre bodies ever read

**Block chosen by `coverage_balance`: `22-Middleware-PhyreEngine`, debt 615 — the largest in the
database, 6,768 functions against 12 documents.** Displaced from the previous block by the stale-parent
failure; run here against the freshly verified `work/headless_gn160/GFZ_GN160`.

New document:
[`../22-Middleware-PhyreEngine/Phyre-Asset-Container/the-cluster-header-and-what-the-loader-rejects.md`](../22-Middleware-PhyreEngine/Phyre-Asset-Container/the-cluster-header-and-what-the-loader-rejects.md).
Documents 203 → 204. **The folder had a README and no document.**

### What was learned

**The complete `.phyre` file header — 21 `uint32` fields, 84 bytes — with byte offsets, recovered
from the engine's own reflection registrations.** The schema had been *counted* before
(`the-file-schema-against-the-runtime-schema.md` reports "`ClusterHeader` → 18 recovered fields") and
**never listed**: no document in the database contained a single cluster-header field name.

**And the loader contract, which is the thing only this database can supply.** `0059A3B0`, 589 B,
read in full:

- 84 bytes read unconditionally; short read → **9**.
- Marker must be `0x50485952` (`RYHP` on disk). **If it is not, the loader byte-swaps the whole
  header and re-tests — so this build accepts big-endian `.phyre` files** and carries a flag saying
  it did.
- `m_platformID` must equal `0x44583131` (`11XD`), `m_physicsEngineID` must equal `0x424C4C54`
  (`TLLB` — **Bullet**) **or be zero**. Both gated on a `checkPlatform` argument that the sole caller
  passes as the **literal 1** (`0059E129`), so the checks always run.
- Header sizes other than 84 are **tolerated in both directions** — shorter headers spill into the
  namespace buffer, longer ones allocate and read the remainder. Both branches are dead against
  shipped data.
- The caller pre-seeds the buffer with `RYHP`/84 before reading over it, so a failed read leaves a
  valid-looking header rather than stack garbage.

**Corpus check, 169 extracted files:** marker, header size, platform and physics id identical in
169/169; and the three D3D11 fields partition the sample cleanly — **6 meshes** (index+vertex),
**70 textures** (texture only), **93 neither**, with **no file carrying both**. The three sizes are a
kind discriminator, not a generic capacity triple. **169 is 0.47 % of the 35,984 shipped and the
document says so in the evidence table.**

### ⚠ Two traps, one avoided by a tag and one by reading the body

**The word "cluster" belongs to two middlewares in this binary.** The hunt began from the string
`\t\t\tError parsing next block of cluster\n`, which xrefs to `00679BF0` — tagged
`CodeDomain.Media.Video.WebM_MKV.Parser`, sitting among `:UNKNOWN TRACK TYPE`,
`PVideoPlaybackWin32::_ReadingThread` and `Clipping in frame %ld`. **That is the Matroska Cluster
element, not a Phyre cluster.** Decompiling it and writing it up as the Phyre loader would have
produced a confidently wrong document about the most-modded file format in the game, and the
disqualifying evidence was in my own string dump three lines above the hit.

> **A shared noun is not a shared subsystem.** The ledger's code-domain tag caught it; the string
> neighbourhood would have caught it too, had it been read rather than skimmed for the one line
> being searched for.

**And a byte census would have inverted the endianness finding.** `0x52594850` (`PHYR`, the marker
byte-reversed) occurs **zero** times in `.text`, against 7 sites for `RYHP` as a positive control.
The natural reading — *"this build refuses big-endian files"* — is **false**. There is no second
constant because the loader **swaps the data and re-tests the same one**. *An absent constant is
evidence about spelling, not about capability* — the third time this session that rule has decided a
question, after MT19937's tempering masks and the Bow formula's `1/218`.

### ⚠ And one fabricated address, caught before the gates

The draft's key-address table gave `00598F80` as the `PInstanceListHeader` registration. **There is
no such registration; it is `00598DF0`.** The other two addresses in that group were read out of the
recovered dataset and the third was filled in by pattern — three registrations, three plausible
neighbouring addresses, and only two of them looked up.

> **The fabricated one sat in a column of real ones and looked exactly like them.** `check_links` and
> `check_consistency` would both have passed it: it is not a broken link, and an address stated once
> agrees with itself. Only looking it up finds it. **Same family as yesterday's 288-byte size —
> a value that entered by expectation rather than by measurement.**

### Not examined

The packed namespace reader (`0059A6F0`, 116 B) — `CU-1`, and possibly a static route to the question
`the-file-schema-against-the-runtime-schema.md` declared statically unanswerable. The six fixup
tables (`CU-3`), which is where cluster *editing* lives. 6,764 of the category's 6,768 functions.

### Gates

`check_links`, `check_consistency`, `check_questions`, `check_formats`, `check_provenance` — green.
`phyre` remains dispositioned `external`, owned by `the-file-schema-against-the-runtime-schema.md`;
**this document deliberately does not re-declare it**, since a second claim on the same unit is
reported as a collision.

---

## 2026-09-07 — `xiionline-61` — the runtime service surface, and two censuses that were wrong the same way

**Block: `24-Compiler-And-CRT-Runtime`, named by `coverage_balance` at debt 572.** New document:
[`../24-Compiler-And-CRT-Runtime/Runtime-Service-Surface/what-the-game-asks-windows-for.md`](../24-Compiler-And-CRT-Runtime/Runtime-Service-Surface/what-the-game-asks-windows-for.md).
Documents 204 → 205.

### How the block was chosen, and the two dead ends first

The category's compiler/EH/static side is well covered by eight documents, so the search was for a
**runtime service** it does not cover. Two candidates were tested and dropped on the evidence:

- **Text encoding.** `MultiByteToWideChar` is **not in the import table at all** and
  `WideCharToMultiByte` has **2 sites in 1 function**. There is no Win32 text-conversion system to
  document. *(The finding survives as a paragraph: conversion goes through `std::codecvt`, and the
  game's encoding is its own.)*
- **CRT `rand`.** Already documented, 48 sites / 30 functions, in `rng-streams.md`.

**Reporting a dead end as a dead end is the point.** Neither became a thin document.

### What was learned

**The retail build ships its developer diagnostics intact, and they are bigger than the API census
shows.**

- `OutputDebugStringA`: **150 direct sites in 93 functions** — 90 audio (SEAD), 2 Steam, and **one
  printf-style wrapper**.
- **`006DF480` is that wrapper**: shadow-spills four argument registers, formats into an `0x800`
  buffer, calls the API at `006DF4FA`. It has **800 call sites from 160 functions**.
- **The two populations do not intersect at all.** **253 functions narrate.**

**`H-3` is answered, and the answer is yes.** Re-identifying PPFX by seven diagnostic-string prefixes
gives 102 functions; **90 of them call the wrapper.** The wrapper's callers span
`006DA8F0`–`00790160`, containing the `0x782289` the PPFX document reports. **PPFX/Yebis narrates its
failures live** — it just never calls the Windows API by name.

**And the crash handler is real.** `005CCD00` (926 B, `Phyre.DebugUtils.AddressInformation`)
symbolises and walks the stack; `MiniDumpWriteDump` is reached from `005CCB39` and `0079ADCA`.

### ⚠ The same mistake twice in one block, in two disguises

**Both censuses I ran undercounted by missing an intermediary, and both would have published a
confident false negative.**

1. **The wrapper.** Counting `OutputDebugStringA` at the API says post-processing is silent. It is
   not; it logs through `006DF480`. **A factor of eight, hidden behind one `call`.**
2. **The thunks.** A scan for `call qword ptr [rip+disp]` returns **zero** sites for
   `MiniDumpWriteDump` and **zero** for `GetAdaptersInfo`. Both zeros are real. Both conclusions —
   *"linked in and dead"* — are false: each is reached through a **`jmp qword ptr [rip+disp]` import
   thunk** with two callers each. `data_xref_scan.py` matches both addressing forms and found them
   immediately; my call-only scan could not.

> **This database already documents this hazard** —
> [`the-thunk-layer-and-why-call-counts-disagree.md`](../24-Compiler-And-CRT-Runtime/Thunk-Layer/the-thunk-layer-and-why-call-counts-disagree.md)
> exists for precisely it, and `00377BA0`'s "zero callers" was the same trap in September. **Knowing
> the rule did not stop me writing the scan that violates it**, because the scan looked like it was
> asking "who calls this?" when it was really asking "who calls this *in one particular encoding*".
>
> **The fix that generalises: never state an absence from a scan you wrote yourself without running
> the sanctioned scanner beside it.** `data_xref_scan.py` demands a control for exactly this reason,
> and the control (`OutputDebugStringA`, 150 refs) passed in both runs — the instrument was sound and
> was simply not the one I had reached for first.

**The corrected finding is strictly better than the one it replaced.** "Retail build ships a dead
minidump writer" is a curiosity; "retail build ships a *working* crash handler" is something a modder
chasing a crash can use tonight.

### Not examined

The message text itself — **only 2 of 150 direct sites pass a literal string**, so the 800 wrapper
call sites' format arguments hold the payload and are unread (`CV-1`). Whether the channel is gated
(`CV-2`). Whether the crash handler is wired to an exception filter (`CV-3`). **The 121 import slots
with no `FF 15` site — which, after this block, may not be called unused until the thunk-aware scan
has covered all of them** (`CV-4`).

### Gates

`check_links`, `check_consistency`, `check_questions`, `check_provenance` — green. `H-3` ticked in
the PPFX document and marked ANSWERED in the register, consistently.

## 2026-09-07 — `xiionline-61` — the packed namespace: every `.phyre` file ships its own schema

**Block: `22-Middleware-PhyreEngine` again, named by `coverage_balance` at debt 564**, and inside it
the highest-value open row, `CU-1`. New document:
[`../22-Middleware-PhyreEngine/Phyre-Asset-Container/the-packed-namespace-and-the-file-side-schema.md`](../22-Middleware-PhyreEngine/Phyre-Asset-Container/the-packed-namespace-and-the-file-side-schema.md).
Documents 205 → 206.

### What was learned

**Every shipped `.phyre` file carries a plain-ASCII list of its own classes and fields**, immediately
after the 84-byte header. `FUN_0059A6F0` (116 B) is the whole reader and it is almost nothing: **one
bulk read of `m_packedNamespaceSize` bytes, error `9` on a short read, a byte-swap fixup through
`0059C180` when the header said big-endian, and no validation of any kind.**

- The block opens with its **own** byte-order mark `04 03 02 01` and repeats its size — **169/169
  files on both**. That is a second endianness statement, independent of the header's `RYHP`.
- The tail is a NUL-separated pool in three groups: type names, class names alphabetically, field
  names in declaration order. **107 type/class names, 236 field names, 42 other tokens.**
- **42 of the tokens are not Phyre names at all** — `CD3D11_BLEND_DESC`, `DepthWriteMask`,
  `CullMode`, `bool`, `float`: Direct3D SDK identifiers, in 99 of 169 files.

**The strongest result is a confirmation nobody arranged.** The namespace of every file names
`m_phyreMarker`, `m_size`, `m_packedNamespaceSize`, `m_platformID` and the rest — **the same 18
fields recovered this morning from the binary's reflection registrations at `005983E0`.** One list
comes from the code that registers the fields, the other from the data files that use them.
**Neither was derived from the other and they agree exactly.**

### The file-side/runtime-side join, quantified

Of the 107 names the files declare, **68 have at least one field registration in the binary and 39 do
not** — but **10 of those 39 are primitives and containers and 7 are enum-shaped**, which have no
fields by construction. **The real gap is 22 structural classes, plus 47 field names.**

**They are not scattered.** Every one is mesh, skeleton, shader or D3D11 resource — `PDataBlock`,
`PShaderVertexProgram`, `PStreamInputLayoutD3D11`, `PTextureCubeMapD3D11`; `m_vertexData`,
`m_skeletonMatrices`, `m_skinBones`, `m_defaultPose`, `m_compiledCode`. **That is precisely the
boundary `the-file-schema-against-the-runtime-schema.md` §7 asks about** — *"why the recovery's
coverage falls off precisely at the shader and D3D11-resource boundary"* — seen from the file side.

**The correspondence is established. The cause is not**, and the document says so: no constructor for
any of the 22 has been read, so "platform resource classes are built by hand-written D3D11 code
rather than by reflection" is filed as a reading at tier 8, not a result.

### ⚠ A count that exceeded its own denominator

The first census reported **`PArray` in 1941 files** — out of a corpus of **169**. The counter was
incremented per *occurrence* while the set it was built from was per *file*.

> **This one announced itself: a subset cannot be larger than the set.** It is the cheapest class of
> check there is and it caught the bug in one glance — but only because the denominator happened to
> be in the same block of output. **A per-file count and a per-occurrence count differ by a factor
> nobody can eyeball once the numbers are both plausible**, and had the corpus been 35,984 instead of
> 169, "1,941 files" would have read as a perfectly reasonable finding and gone straight into a
> document.
>
> **Print the denominator next to every ratio**, so the impossible case stays impossible to miss.

### ⚠ And a stale premise of my own, corrected

`CU-1` — filed by me two blocks ago — described this as *"the exact question
`the-file-schema-against-the-runtime-schema.md` declared statically unanswerable"*. **That was
already false when I wrote it.** That document had been **reopened and corrected earlier the same
day**: its §3b now records the "not statically answerable" conclusion as an error, refuted by
`xiionline-8d`. I quoted a claim from a document I had not re-read, in a database whose whole point
is that claims move. **The row has been corrected in place with the correction visible.**

### Not examined

**The table between `+0x08` and the string pool — two thirds of the block, and the part that actually
maps a name to an `m_classID`** (`CW-1`). `FUN_0059C180`, which must encode that table's layout to
swap it, and is therefore the likely cheapest route in (`CW-2`).

### Gates

`check_links`, `check_consistency`, `check_questions` — green.

## 2026-09-07 — `xiionline-61` — the `.phyre` schema table decoded, and a prediction that held

**Block: `22-Middleware-PhyreEngine` (debt 521, top of `coverage_balance`), on `CW-1`** — the highest
open item in the database. Extension, not a new document: §8 of
[`../22-Middleware-PhyreEngine/Phyre-Asset-Container/the-packed-namespace-and-the-file-side-schema.md`](../22-Middleware-PhyreEngine/Phyre-Asset-Container/the-packed-namespace-and-the-file-side-schema.md).

**`CW-2` named the route and the route worked.** It said `FUN_0059C180`, the namespace byte-swap
fixup, *"has to know the table's field layout to swap it correctly, so it is probably the cheapest
route to `CW-1`: a swap routine is a structure description written as code."* **202 bytes of `bswap`
gave up the entire layout.**

### The layout, and how hard it is checked

```
+0x00 BOM 0x01020304 | +0x04 namespaceSize | +0x08 nTypes | +0x0C nClasses
+0x10 nMembers       | +0x14 stringPoolSize | +0x18 0 | +0x1C 0
+0x20 u32[nTypes] | class[9 words] x nClasses | member[6 words] x nMembers | char[stringPoolSize]
```

- **`0x20 + 4T + 36C + 24M + S == m_packedNamespaceSize` in 169/169 files, to the byte.**
- **`sum(class[w3]) == nMembers` in 169/169** — so word 3 is the member count and members are stored
  contiguously in class order. **That is the whole mapping**: every class, every field, its byte
  offset, its size and whether it is a pointer, reconstructable from the namespace alone.
- Class word 2 and member word 0 are name offsets — **9,203/9,203 and 27,543/27,543**, tested by
  requiring the value to land on the *first byte* of a NUL-terminated name in every record of every
  file.

### ⚠ A column test that passed for the wrong reason

Four class columns and one member column first reported "always a name". **They are always
*zero*, and pool offset 0 is a legitimate name** — the first string. **A column of zeros passes a
validity test trivially.**

> The re-run excluded zero values and the four columns collapsed to `0 resolve / 0 do not` — unused,
> not naming. **The test was right; the population it ran on included a value that satisfies it
> vacuously.** The same shape as a control that only ever proves a positive: *a predicate is not
> evidence until you know the input could have failed it.*

### A third independent confirmation of the header layout

Member word 2 is the field's byte offset within its class. For `PClusterHeaderBase` it reads
`m_phyreMarker` 0, `m_size` 4, `m_packedNamespaceSize` 8, `m_platformID` 12 … `m_physicsEngineID`
68 — **the map recovered this morning from the binary's reflection registrations.**

> **3,042 offset checks across 169 files. Zero mismatches.** The cluster header now rests on **three
> sources that could each have been wrong alone**: the code that registers the fields, the code that
> reads the file, and the files themselves.

### ⚠ The binary registers a field no shipped file declares

`PInstanceListHeader` is registered with **ten** fields, ending `m_pointerArrayFixupSize` at `+0x24`.
**Every one of the 169 files declares nine**, stopping at `+0x20`. **169/169, no exceptions.**

Filed as `CX-1` rather than resolved. It matters practically: **a tool that trusts the runtime layout
to parse a shipped file reads four bytes that are not there.**

### Not examined

`CX-2` — the class→`m_classID` link, the last piece between the namespace and walking a cluster's
data. Two class words remain unidentified, and `w0` is **not** a type-table index (in range for only
6,199 of 9,203). `CX-3`, the member type reference, which indexes the type table for primitives and
is out of range for others.

### Gates

`check_links`, `check_consistency`, `check_questions` — green. §2's three statements that the table
was undecoded were **reconciled with §8** rather than left standing, and §5's `CW-1`/`CW-2` boxes
ticked with their register rows updated to match. Decoder kept as
`analysis-kit/gfz-scripts/decode_phyre_namespace.py`.

---

## 2026-09-07 — `xiionline-61` — falsification pass over the day's own four documents

**Block chosen against `coverage_balance`, deliberately.** The tool named `22-Middleware-PhyreEngine`
for a third time in four blocks. **Four documents had been authored today and none audited**,
`audit_coverage` had fallen every block (80.30 → 79.13 %), and the session had a demonstrated error
rate — five real errors caught, several within one step of publication. **A document written today by
a session that made those mistakes is the highest-value thing to attack.**

`audit_coverage` **79.13 % → 81.07 %** (163 → 167 of 206). Questions closed 358 → 360.

### The systematic attack

**Every "only caller" / "N callers" claim in the four documents was made with a scan matching
`E8 call rel32` only.** The CRT block later established that this binary reaches things through
`E9 jmp rel32` tail calls and import thunks. **So every such claim was re-run against both forms**,
plus `data_xref_scan.py` for address-taken references, with a passing control.

Ten targets. **Nine stood. One did not.**

### ⚠ Finding 1 — the document that taught the lesson contained an instance of it

`what-the-game-asks-windows-for.md` is the document that established that a call-only scan misses
`jmp` thunks. **Its own wrapper census was call-only.** There is an `E9` tail call into `006DF480` at
`00788471`: **801 sites, not 800.**

The function count of 160 is unaffected — that function already appears via its own `call` at
`00788460` — so **no narrator was missed and the headline 253 stands.** Corrected in four places.

> **Writing the rule down is not the same as having applied it everywhere.** The audit is what
> applies it.

### ⚠ Finding 2 — an invented discrepancy, withdrawn

Published this morning in two documents: *"`PInstanceListHeader` is registered with **ten** fields and
every one of the 169 shipped files declares **nine**"* — filed as `CX-1`, with a practical warning
that a tool trusting the runtime layout would read four bytes that are not there.

**The registration site `00598DF0` has nine rows. Both sides say nine. There was never a
discrepancy.**

**The tenth field was manufactured by my own grep.** The class's registrations were pulled with a
case-insensitive search for `InstanceList`, which also matches **`m_instanceListCount` — a field of
`PClusterHeaderBase`** — and that row was counted into this class's list.

> **This is a worse failure than a miscount, and worth separating from one.** A miscount makes a true
> statement imprecise. **This created a conflict between two sources that agree, supplied it with a
> plausible cause** ("the exporter predates the field"), **attached an actionable warning to it, and
> propagated it into a second document and the question register.**
>
> **Nothing downstream could have caught it.** The number was self-consistent; `check_consistency`
> sees one address described one way; the story was reasonable. **Only going back to the registration
> site finds it.**
>
> **The rule: a class's field list must be anchored on the class, never on a substring of its name.**
> `m_instanceListCount` and `PInstanceListHeader` share eleven characters and nothing else.

Corrections applied: the field table and heading in
`the-cluster-header-and-what-the-loader-rejects.md` (ten fields / "40-byte header" → **nine fields /
36-byte header**), the §8 section and §5 checkbox in
`the-packed-namespace-and-the-file-side-schema.md`, and `CX-1` marked **REFUTED** rather than closed.

### What stood

- `0059A3B0` **exactly one caller** — 1 `call`, **0 `jmp`**, and **0 address-taken references** against
  a control of 150. Stronger than when it was written.
- All five MP-document caller claims — `00307300`, `002F91A0`, `002F9240`, `002FB6C0`, `002F9400`.
- The recovery loop's unreachable escape at `002F9524`, re-traced from the instructions rather than
  re-read.
- The D3D11 buffer-size partition, 6 / 70 / 93 summing to 169 with no mixed case.
- The namespace layout arithmetic, 169/169.

### Not attacked, and said so in each document

The `+0x28` = maximum-MP identification, which depends on another document's join; the borrowed
"173 of 1,154 fields"; the per-DLL import counts read straight from the PE; the class→`m_classID`
question; and every function each document already lists as unread.

### Gates

`check_links`, `check_consistency`, `check_questions` — green.

## 2026-09-07 — `xiionline-61` — `CR-1`, and a question I should never have filed

**Block: `01-Script-VM-Native-Bridge`** — the highest **player-facing** debt (317), chosen over
`coverage_balance`'s top row deliberately: **three of the previous five blocks were Phyre**, and the
skill's own warning is that the loop drifts toward whatever technique is currently yielding.
Questions closed 360 → 361; `native-command-lifecycle.md` `audit.passes` 4 → 5.

### The answer

**`CR-1`: a script that calls a verb with an all-null dispatch record gets a silent no-op that cannot
hang.** `VMNativeCommand_ExecuteLifecycle` (`002621D0`) guards each slot independently:

- **`begin` null → skipped** (`002622BF`), falling straight into the poll block.
- **`poll` null → substitutes the local zeroed at entry** (`002621F7 mov dword [rsp+0x88], 0`), so
  `& 1` is false and the command reads **finished**. *The dispatcher's default for a missing poll is
  "done", not "busy" — which is why a stubbed verb can never wedge a zone script.*
- **`result` null → `00262360`**, which pushes tag `1` / kind `6` / value `0` onto the 63-entry
  result list and resets the phase byte to `0`.

**The 63-entry bound fails closed on the push and open on the completion**: at capacity `jge 0x262382`
skips the value but the command still finishes. **Losing a result is preferred to wedging a script.**

So `setnorecovertelepostatus` (`0x0330`), `battlemembersync` (`0x0356`) and `restartbattle`
(`0x03B0`) are safe to call and safe to leave in a script — the same observable outcome as the
retail-stubbed verbs, by a different route: **the stubs complete because their `poll` returns 0;
these complete because there is no `poll` and the default is 0.**

### ⚠ The finding that matters more: the question was already answered when I filed it

**`native-command-lifecycle.md` §2 already said it** — *"where a callback is absent it takes an
alternative path rather than failing"* and *"the absent `poll` yields 'not running' from a zeroed
local"* — in the same category, one directory away, in a document with four falsification passes.

**I filed `CR-1` earlier today anyway**, in the "What is not known" section of a document I was
writing about those very verbs.

> **`check_already_answered.py` exists in the analysis kit for precisely this.** Its docstring opens:
> *"On 2026-09-03, inside about ten minutes, the same failure happened three times"* — a slice told it
> needed a mechanism its own document had already published as its headline finding; a session
> re-deriving an identity the database already held; four properties raised as a live thread that were
> already named. **I did not run it, and I reproduced the failure it was written to stop.**
>
> Run against the question, it answers in one line: **`AO-3`, already ANSWERED, on the phase byte.**
>
> **The rule: run `check_already_answered.py` before filing an open question, not only before
> assigning one.** Filing is where the waste is created — an assignment merely spends it. A question
> in the register is a claim that the database does *not* know something, and that claim needs the
> same evidence as any other.

**The block still produced something real:** the `00262360` branch was not written down anywhere, and
composing the three guards into "an all-null record completes in one call" required all three. But
**the honest split is one new branch against a question that should have been closed on filing**, and
that is how it is recorded — in the register row, in the document checkbox, and here.

I also checked the other questions filed today — `CR-2`, `CS-5`, `CV-5`, `CX-2`, `CX-1` — against the
same tool. **Every remaining hit is one of my own documents from today; none is a prior answer.** The
CR-1 case was the only one.

### Not examined

`CR-2` (what `restartbattle` *did*) is untouched and explicitly not answered by this — §14 establishes
only what calling it now does. `CR-3` and `CR-4` unchanged.

### Gates

`check_links`, `check_consistency`, `check_questions` — green.

## 2026-09-07 — `xiionline-61` — the verb reach map, and the same substring bug twice in one day

**Block: `01-Script-VM-Native-Bridge`, second consecutive** — the highest player-facing debt, and the
category where a modder's actual question lives. New document:
[`../01-Script-VM-Native-Bridge/Verb-Dispatch/what-each-script-verb-touches.md`](../01-Script-VM-Native-Bridge/Verb-Dispatch/what-each-script-verb-touches.md),
**`status: PARTIAL`, tier 3**, with
[`scriptvm-verb-reach.tsv`](../01-Script-VM-Native-Bridge/Verb-Dispatch/scriptvm-verb-reach.tsv) —
1,960 rows. Documents 206 → 207.

### Why this was worth building

**The verb table already had an `owner` column and it answers nothing**: 1,857 of 1,888 rows say
`Script_VM_Native_Bridge`, which is the handler's own category. **What a script modder wants is which
subsystem a verb disturbs**, and that has to come from the handler's callees.

**616 of 1,960 verbs reach a named non-bridge subsystem at depth 1** — Movement 182, ActorEntity 142,
Party 51, Presentation 42, Audio 36, Input 35, and a long tail. **Movement and ActorEntity are more
than half: a zone script is largely a machine for putting bodies in places and asking them
questions.**

**This assigns ownership to nothing.** The standing rule forbids inferring an owner from a caller;
the document says so explicitly. Every handler here is already bridge-owned — that is *why* the owner
tag is useless — and what is reported is which owners the handler *calls into*, a fact about edges.

### Depth is not free, and the map says where it stops

`Save_Load_Settings` is reached by **0** verbs at depth 1, **28** at depth 2, **61** at depth 3. **No
script verb does save/load.** A subsystem that appears only on the second hop is the signature of a
shared helper, so **the published map is depth 1 only** — deeper hops measure the engine's
connectedness rather than the verb's purpose.

Discounting callees by in-degree lifts control agreement **65 % → 71 %** (at ≤ 20, labelling 458
instead of 616). **The gain is modest, which is the finding: the shared-helper problem is real and
not dominant.** Both columns ship in the TSV so a reader can see when a label depends on the filter.

### ⚠ The same unanchored-substring bug as this morning, in a different costume

The control scored verb names that imply a subsystem against the computed reach. **It first reported
196 disagreements**, with rows like *"`setvelr` — name says Audio"*, *"`setrect` — name says Audio"*,
*"`releasepc` — name says Audio"*.

**The keyword list contained `'se'`.** It matched inside **set**, **use** and **relea<u>se</u>**.

> **This is the second unanchored-substring failure in one day.** This morning a case-insensitive
> grep for `InstanceList` matched `m_instanceListCount` — a field of a different class — and
> manufactured a schema discrepancy that reached two documents and the register.
>
> **Both were mine, both were in throwaway analysis code rather than in a document, and both produced
> confident wrong output that looked like a finding.** The first cost a published false claim; this
> one was caught only because `setvelr → Audio` is absurd on sight. **A less absurd pairing would have
> gone through.**
>
> **The rule, now stated twice: a token shorter than three characters is not a search term, and a
> search for a name must be anchored — on a word boundary, a column, or a structure — never on a
> substring.** The corrected list dropped every two-letter token; agreement went 196 disagreements →
> **53**.

### What the control actually measures

**118 agree · 53 disagree · 242 name a subsystem and reach nothing.** Reading the 53 is more useful
than the ratio, because **most favour the graph over the name**: `musicfadeout` reaches Audio (the
`fade` keyword said Presentation), `getpos` reaches ActorEntity because a getter reads an actor
rather than moving one, `getmapjumpposbyindex` reaches WorldAreaField because it queries the map.
**The instrument and the control disagree most often where the control is wrong**, so 69 % is not an
accuracy figure and the document declines to present it as one.

### ⚠ And a worked case where the map is wrong

**`fadein` is labelled `Resource`** — entirely because its result handler `00355EB0` calls
**`001D6C20`, a 21-byte function tagged `ResourceDataLoading`.** A 21-byte callee is an accessor, not
a subsystem. **The in-degree filter does not catch this one.**

Published in the document as the map's failure mode rather than left for a reader to trip over, with
the rule: **treat a single-hit label as a lead, never an answer** — which is why the TSV prints hit
counts, so `Resource:1` can be told from `Movement:3`.

### Not examined

**Not one handler body was read** — the entire map is call graph plus owner tags, which is why the
document is tier 3 and `PARTIAL`. The 1,344 verbs the map cannot label (`CY-1`). Whether a *low*
in-degree second hop would extend it honestly (`CY-2`). And a join nobody has run: whether any verb's
reach contradicts its recorded arity or shape (`CY-4`).

### Gates

`check_links`, `check_consistency`, `check_questions` — green.

## 2026-09-08 — `xiionline-61` — `CY-4` and `CY-1`: why FFXII cutscenes are full of `*sync`

**Block: `01-Script-VM-Native-Bridge`, third consecutive — at the cap, and deliberately.** Both
questions were self-checks on a document published the previous block, both cost one pass over files
already in the tree, and `CY-4` carried real stakes: **if a verb's reach contradicted its recorded
shape, one of two tables was wrong and one of them was an hour old.**

`scriptvm-verb-dispatch-and-parameter-encoding.md` `audit.passes` 3 → 4. Questions closed 362 → 364.

### `CY-4` — no contradiction, and a better finding underneath

**The `shape` column is defined as "`begin` present"**, which is exactly what it records. But §1 of
that document glosses it as *"some verbs take time and hold the script until they are done"* — and
**for 77 verbs that gloss is false.**

| slots | verbs | behaviour |
|---|---:|---|
| `result` only — *instantaneous* | 1,571 | completes in one call |
| `begin` + `poll` — *durative* | **250** | genuinely holds the script |
| **`begin`, no `poll`** — also *durative* | **77** | **completes in one call** |
| `poll` only — *empty* | 29 | holds the script, started by someone else |

**The 77 finish immediately by the rule established two blocks ago**
(`native-command-lifecycle.md` §14, from `CR-1`): **a null `poll` substitutes a zeroed local, and
zero means finished.** A verb with a `begin` and no `poll` runs its begin, is asked "still running?",
and the dispatcher answers "no" on its behalf.

**And that is why the game has `*sync` verbs at all:**

| `rmove` `0x0042` | durative | begin `003488B0` | **no poll** | starts a move, returns at once |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `movesync` `0x003D` | empty | — | poll `00356A90` | waits for it |
| `rturn` `0x0047` | durative | begin `0034B670` | **no poll** | starts a turn, returns at once |
| `turnsync` `0x0046` | empty | — | poll `00341D50` | waits for it |
| `wait` `0x0000` | durative | begin `0034CF90` | poll `0034DCC0` | genuinely blocks |

> **A move verb physically cannot wait — it has no `poll` slot for the dispatcher to ask.** The
> pairing every cutscene uses is not an authoring convention; it is forced by the table.
>
> `verbs-that-only-wait-and-verbs-that-do-nothing.md` established that a `*sync` waits for
> *"something started by an earlier verb"*. **The earlier verb is now identified as a class**, and
> `CR-1`'s dispatcher rule explains why the split has to exist. **Three documents, three blocks, one
> mechanism.**

**The scripting language has three behaviours, not two** — finish, **start-and-return**, block — and
the `shape` vocabulary has a name for only two of them.

Two rows in 1,960 point the other way: `usemoviememory` and `debug_freeiopvoice` are labelled
`instantaneous` and carry a `poll`, so they can block despite the label. Filed `CZ-1`, unread.

### `CY-1` — the map's silence, split four ways, and the dominant cause is not what I guessed

The reach document said the 1,344 unlabelled verbs were unlabelled because *"the handler is thin, or
its callees carry no owner tag"*. **Neither is the main reason.**

| cause | verbs |
|---|---:|
| **every tagged callee is bridge-owned** | **1,029** |
| handler calls nothing — inline body | 218 |
| every callee is untagged | 93 |
| no handler at all | 4 |

> **1,029 handlers call only the value stack, the scratch block and the coercion helpers.** They do
> their real work in their own instructions. **The reach map cannot be extended to them by any amount
> of graph work** — no deeper hop, no better filter, no naming pass.
>
> **Only 93 verbs are silent because the ledger has not named their callees.** That is the one
> category a naming pass would recover, and it is 7 % of the silence rather than the bulk of it.
>
> So the real size of the "what does this verb do" problem is **1,029 handlers that must be read one
> at a time** — filed `CZ-3`. Not 1,344, and not "mostly a tagging gap", which is what I would have
> assumed without the split.

**The reach document's "What is not covered" section has been reconciled with this** rather than left
to contradict it, the same way §2 of the namespace document was reconciled with its §8.

### Not examined

Not one handler body, again — `CY-3` stands untouched. Whether the 77 starts and the 29 waiters pair
one-to-one (`CZ-2`); a start with no waiter would be a verb a script can never synchronise on.

### Gates

`check_links`, `check_consistency`, `check_questions` — green.
